					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Founding Father, 4th president of the United States</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="James Madison (disambiguation)">James Madison (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"President Madison" redirects here. For ships named <i>President Madison</i>, see <a href="/wiki/SS_President_Madison" title="SS President Madison">SS <i>President Madison</i></a>.</div>

<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Dolley_Payne" class="mw-redirect" title="Dolley Payne">Dolley Payne</a></div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1038841319">.mw-parser-output .tooltip-dotted{border-bottom:1px dotted;cursor:help}</style><span class="rt-commentedText tooltip" title="September 15, 1794">1794</span>&#41;<wbr />&#8203;</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Parent</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/James_Madison_Sr." title="James Madison Sr.">James Madison Sr.</a> (father)</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Education</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/College_of_New_Jersey_(18th_century)" class="mw-redirect" title="College of New Jersey (18th century)">College of New Jersey</a> (<a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts" title="Bachelor of Arts">BA</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Madison_sig.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="James Madison&#39;s signature"><img alt="James Madison signature" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/James_Madison_sig.svg/128px-James_Madison_sig.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="25" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/James_Madison_sig.svg/192px-James_Madison_sig.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/James_Madison_sig.svg/256px-James_Madison_sig.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="403" data-file-height="78" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>James Madison</b> (March 16, 1751<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>&#160;&#8211;&#32;June 28, 1836) was an American statesman, diplomat, and <a href="/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States" title="Founding Fathers of the United States">Founding Father</a> who served as the fourth <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1809 to 1817. Madison is hailed as the "<a href="/wiki/James_Madison_as_Father_of_the_Constitution" title="James Madison as Father of the Constitution">Father of the Constitution</a>" for his pivotal role in drafting and promoting the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States" title="Constitution of the United States">Constitution of the United States</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights" title="United States Bill of Rights">Bill of Rights</a>.
</p><p>Madison was born into a prominent <a href="/wiki/Slavery" title="Slavery">slave-owning</a> <a href="/wiki/Planter_class" title="Planter class">planter</a> family in <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a>. He served as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_House_of_Delegates" title="Virginia House of Delegates">Virginia House of Delegates</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Congress" title="Continental Congress">Continental Congress</a> during and after the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>. Dissatisfied with the weak national government established by the <a href="/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation" title="Articles of Confederation">Articles of Confederation</a>, he helped organize the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(United_States)" title="Constitutional Convention (United States)">Constitutional Convention</a>, which produced a new constitution designed to strengthen <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States" title="Republicanism in the United States">republican</a> government against <a href="/wiki/Direct_democracy" title="Direct democracy">democratic</a> assembly. Madison's <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Plan" title="Virginia Plan">Virginia Plan</a> was the basis for the convention's deliberations, and he was an influential voice at the convention. He became one of the leaders in the movement to ratify the Constitution and joined <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Alexander Hamilton</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Jay" title="John Jay">John Jay</a> in writing <i><a href="/wiki/The_Federalist_Papers" title="The Federalist Papers">The Federalist Papers</a></i>, a series of pro-ratification essays that remains prominent among works of political science in American history. Madison emerged as an important leader in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> and was a close adviser to President <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a>.
</p><p>During the early 1790s, Madison opposed the economic program and the accompanying centralization of power favored by <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a> Hamilton. Alongside <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a>, he organized the <a href="/wiki/Democratic%E2%80%93Republican_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic–Republican Party">Democratic–Republican Party</a> in opposition to Hamilton's <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist Party</a>. After Jefferson was <a href="/wiki/1800_United_States_presidential_election" title="1800 United States presidential election">elected president in 1800</a>, Madison served as his Secretary of State from 1801 to 1809 and supported Jefferson in the case of <i><a href="/wiki/Marbury_v._Madison" title="Marbury v. Madison">Marbury v. Madison</a></i>. While Madison was Secretary of State, Jefferson made the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>, and later, as President, Madison oversaw related disputes in the Northwest Territories.
</p><p>Madison was <a href="/wiki/1808_United_States_presidential_election" title="1808 United States presidential election">elected president in 1808</a>. Motivated by desire to acquire land held by Britain, Spain, and Native Americans, and after diplomatic protests with a trade embargo failed to end <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">British</a> seizures of American shipped goods, Madison led the United States into the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>. Although the war ended inconclusively, many Americans viewed the war's outcome as a successful "second war of independence" against Britain. Madison was <a href="/wiki/1812_United_States_presidential_election" title="1812 United States presidential election">re-elected in 1812</a>, albeit by a smaller margin. The war convinced Madison of the necessity of a stronger federal government. He presided over the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> and the enactment of the <a href="/wiki/Protectionism_in_the_United_States" title="Protectionism in the United States">protective</a> <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1816" title="Tariff of 1816">Tariff of 1816</a>. By treaty or through war, <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Native American tribes</a> ceded 26,000,000 acres (11,000,000&#160;ha) of land to the United States under Madison's presidency.
</p><p>Retiring from public office at the end of his presidency in 1817, Madison returned to his plantation, <a href="/wiki/Montpelier_(Orange,_Virginia)" title="Montpelier (Orange, Virginia)">Montpelier</a>, and died there in 1836. During his lifetime, Madison was a slave owner. In 1783, to prevent a slave rebellion at Montpelier, Madison freed one of his slaves. He did not free any slaves in his will. Among historians, Madison is considered one of the most important Founding Fathers of the United States. Leading <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">historians have generally ranked</a> him as an above-average president, although they are critical of his endorsement of slavery and his leadership during the War of 1812. Madison's name is commemorated in many landmarks across the nation, both publicly and privately, with prominent examples including <a href="/wiki/Madison_Square_Garden" title="Madison Square Garden">Madison Square Garden</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_University" title="James Madison University">James Madison University</a>, the <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_Memorial_Building" title="James Madison Memorial Building">James Madison Memorial Building</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/USS_James_Madison" title="USS James Madison">USS&#160;<i>James Madison</i></a>.
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-4 .toclevel-3 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-5 .toclevel-4 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-6 .toclevel-5 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-7 .toclevel-6 ul{display:none}</style><div class="toclimit-3"><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" /></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<p>James Madison Jr. was born on March 16, 1751 (March 5, 1750, <a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">Old Style</a>), at <a href="/wiki/Belle_Grove_(Port_Conway,_Virginia)" title="Belle Grove (Port Conway, Virginia)">Belle Grove</a> Plantation near <a href="/wiki/Port_Conway,_Virginia" title="Port Conway, Virginia">Port Conway</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia" title="Colony of Virginia">Colony of Virginia</a>, to <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_Sr." title="James Madison Sr.">James Madison Sr.</a> and Eleanor Rose Conway Madison. His family had lived in Virginia since the mid-17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Madison's maternal grandfather, Francis Conway, was a prominent planter and <a href="/wiki/Tobacco" title="Tobacco">tobacco</a> merchant.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham19905_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham19905-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> His father was a tobacco planter who grew up on a <a href="/wiki/Plantations_in_the_American_South" class="mw-redirect" title="Plantations in the American South">plantation</a>, then called <a href="/wiki/Montpelier_(James_Madison)" class="mw-redirect" title="Montpelier (James Madison)">Mount Pleasant</a>, which he inherited upon reaching adulthood. With an estimated 100 <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slaves</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> and a 5,000-acre (2,000&#160;ha) plantation, Madison's father was among the largest landowners in Virginia's <a href="/wiki/Piedmont_(United_States)" title="Piedmont (United States)">Piedmont</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier_Foundation_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier_Foundation-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early 1760s, the Madison family moved into a newly built house that they named Montpelier.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Madison grew up as the oldest of twelve children,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham199012_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham199012-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> with seven brothers and four sisters, though only six lived to adulthood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Of the surviving three brothers (Francis, Ambrose, and William) and three sisters (Nelly, Sarah, and Frances), it was Ambrose who would eventually help to manage Montpelier for both his father and older brother until his own death in 1793.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003370–371_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003370–371-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> President <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a> was a descendant of Elder <a href="/wiki/William_Brewster_(Pilgrim)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Brewster (Pilgrim)">William Brewster</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Pilgrim_(Plymouth_Colony)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pilgrim (Plymouth Colony)">Pilgrim</a> leader of the <a href="/wiki/Plymouth_Colony" title="Plymouth Colony">Plymouth Colony</a>, a <i><a href="/wiki/Mayflower" title="Mayflower">Mayflower</a></i> immigrant, and a signer of the <a href="/wiki/Mayflower_Compact" title="Mayflower Compact">Mayflower Compact</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Allerton_Jr." title="Isaac Allerton Jr.">Isaac Allerton Jr.</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Colonial_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Colonial America">colonial</a> merchant, colonel, and son of <i>Mayflower</i> Pilgrim <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Allerton" title="Isaac Allerton">Isaac Allerton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fear_Brewster" title="Fear Brewster">Fear Brewster</a>. Taylor's second cousin through that line was Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194122,_259_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194122,_259-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Madison&#39;s birthplace. It is on the west side of US 301 in front of Emmanuel Episcopal Church and is just north of the Rappahannock River bridge. Belle Grove plantation house, the actual birthplace, was located 400 yards east and is no longer in existence" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg/220px-MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg/330px-MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg/440px-MadisonBirthplacePortConwayVA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1458" data-file-height="1020" /></a><figcaption>Virginia historic marker for Birthplace of President James Madison in Port Conway, Virginia</figcaption></figure>
<p>From age 11 to 16, Madison studied under Donald Robertson, a Scottish instructor who served as a tutor for several prominent planter families in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">South</a>. Madison learned mathematics, geography, and modern and classical languages, becoming exceptionally proficient in <a href="/wiki/Classical_Latin" title="Classical Latin">Latin</a>. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoyd,_2013_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd,_2013-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> At age 16, Madison returned to Montpelier, where he studied under the Reverend Thomas Martin to prepare for college. Unlike most college-bound Virginians of his day, Madison did not attend the <a href="/wiki/College_of_William_and_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="College of William and Mary">College of William and Mary</a>, where the lowland <a href="/wiki/Williamsburg,_Virginia" title="Williamsburg, Virginia">Williamsburg</a> climate—thought to be more likely to harbor infectious disease—might have strained his sensibilities concerning his own health.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGutzman20122_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutzman20122-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Instead, in 1769, he enrolled at the College of New Jersey (later renamed <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman20173–7_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman20173–7-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1811,_Sharples,_James,_James_Madison.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Madison as a young man at Princeton" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/1811%2C_Sharples%2C_James%2C_James_Madison.jpg/220px-1811%2C_Sharples%2C_James%2C_James_Madison.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="269" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/1811%2C_Sharples%2C_James%2C_James_Madison.jpg/330px-1811%2C_Sharples%2C_James%2C_James_Madison.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/1811%2C_Sharples%2C_James%2C_James_Madison.jpg/440px-1811%2C_Sharples%2C_James%2C_James_Madison.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1633" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>Madison as a student at Princeton, portrait by <a href="/wiki/James_Sharples_(portrait_painter)" title="James Sharples (portrait painter)">James Sharples</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>His college studies included Latin, Greek, theology, and the works of the <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment" title="Age of Enlightenment">Enlightenment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman20175–6_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman20175–6-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Emphasis was placed on both speech and debate; Madison was a leading member of the <a href="/wiki/American_Whig%E2%80%93Cliosophic_Society" title="American Whig–Cliosophic Society">American Whig–Cliosophic Society</a>, which competed on campus with a political counterpart, the Cliosophic Society.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200335_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200335-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> During his time at Princeton, Madison's closest friend was future Attorney General <a href="/wiki/William_Bradford_(Attorney_General)" title="William Bradford (Attorney General)">William Bradford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200334_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200334-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Along with fellow classmate <a href="/wiki/Aaron_Burr" title="Aaron Burr">Aaron Burr</a>, Madison undertook an intense program of study and completed the college's three-year Bachelor of Arts degree in two years, graduating in 1771.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman20174–5_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman20174–5-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Madison had contemplated either entering the clergy or practicing law after graduation but instead remained at Princeton to study Hebrew and <a href="/wiki/Political_philosophy" title="Political philosophy">political philosophy</a> under the college's president, <a href="/wiki/John_Witherspoon" title="John Witherspoon">John Witherspoon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He returned home to Montpelier in early 1772.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham199051_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham199051-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison's ideas on philosophy and morality were strongly shaped by Witherspoon, who converted him to the philosophy, values, and modes of thinking of the Age of Enlightenment. Biographer Terence Ball wrote that at Princeton, Madison "was immersed in the liberalism of the Enlightenment, and converted to eighteenth-century political radicalism. From then on James Madison's theories would advance the rights of happiness of man, and his most active efforts would serve devotedly the cause of civil and political liberty."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBall201745–46_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall201745–46-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After returning to Montpelier, without a chosen career, Madison served as a tutor to his younger siblings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman20177–8_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman20177–8-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Madison began to study law books in 1773, asking his friend Bradford, a law apprentice, to send him a written plan of study. He had acquired an understanding of legal publications by 1783. Madison saw himself as a law student but not a lawyer; he did not apprentice himself to a lawyer and never joined the <a href="/wiki/Bar_(law)" title="Bar (law)">bar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBilder2010390–391_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBilder2010390–391-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Following the Revolutionary War, Madison spent time at Montpelier in Virginia studying ancient democracies of the world in preparation for the Constitutional Convention.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCullough200621_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCullough200621-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Madison suffered from episodes of mental exhaustion and illness with associated nervousness, which often caused temporary short-term incapacity after periods of stress. However, he enjoyed good physical health until his final years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200258_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200258-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="American_Revolution_and_Articles_of_Confederation">American Revolution and Articles of Confederation</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a> and <a href="/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation" title="Articles of Confederation">Articles of Confederation</a></div>
<p>During the 1760s and 1770s, American Colonists protested tightened British tax, monetary, and military laws forced on them by Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor2002442–443_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2002442–443-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> In 1765, the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Great_Britain" title="Parliament of Great Britain">British Parliament</a> passed the <a href="/wiki/Stamp_Act_1765" title="Stamp Act 1765">Stamp Act</a>, which caused strong opposition by the colonists and began a conflict that would culminate in the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIsaacson2004_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsaacson2004-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor201696–100_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor201696–100-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The American Revolutionary War broke out on April 19, 1775 and was ended by the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1783)" title="Treaty of Paris (1783)">Treaty of Paris</a> signed on September 3, 1783.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIsaacson2004_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsaacson2004-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor2016132–135_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2016132–135-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPatersonCliffordMaddock201420_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatersonCliffordMaddock201420-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The colonists formed three prominent factions: <a href="/wiki/Loyalist_(American_Revolution)" title="Loyalist (American Revolution)">Loyalists</a>, who continued to back King <a href="/wiki/George_III" title="George III">George&#160;III</a> of the United Kingdom; a significant neutral faction without firm commitments to either Loyalists or Patriots; and the <a href="/wiki/Patriot_(American_Revolution)" title="Patriot (American Revolution)">Patriots</a>, whom Madison joined, under the leadership of the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Congress" title="Continental Congress">Continental Congress</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJillsonWilson19944_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJillsonWilson19944-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor2016135–139,_123–127_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2016135–139,_123–127-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Madison believed that Parliament had overstepped its bounds by attempting to tax the American colonies, and he sympathized with those who resisted British rule.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201717–18_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201717–18-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Historically, debate about the consecration of bishops was ongoing and eventual legislation was passed in the British Parliament (subsequently called the Consecration of Bishops Abroad Act 1786) to allow bishops to be consecrated for an American church outside of allegiance to the British Crown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards198443_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards198443-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Both in the United States and in Canada, the new Anglican churches began incorporating more active forms of polity in their own self-government, collective decision-making, and self-supported financing; these measures would be consistent with separation of religious and secular identities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards1984322_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards1984322-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Madison believed these measures to be insufficient, and also favored <a href="/wiki/Disestablishment" class="mw-redirect" title="Disestablishment">disestablishing</a> the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican Church">Anglican Church</a> in Virginia; Madison believed that tolerance of an established religion was detrimental not only to <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_religion" title="Freedom of religion">freedom of religion</a> but also because it encouraged excessive deference to any authority which might be asserted by an established church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201710–14_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201710–14-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Madison,_by_Charles_Willson_Peale,_1783.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Madison portrait as a young man." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/James_Madison%2C_by_Charles_Willson_Peale%2C_1783.png/170px-James_Madison%2C_by_Charles_Willson_Peale%2C_1783.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/James_Madison%2C_by_Charles_Willson_Peale%2C_1783.png/255px-James_Madison%2C_by_Charles_Willson_Peale%2C_1783.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/James_Madison%2C_by_Charles_Willson_Peale%2C_1783.png/340px-James_Madison%2C_by_Charles_Willson_Peale%2C_1783.png 2x" data-file-width="1272" data-file-height="1700" /></a><figcaption>Madison's portrait as Congressional delegate at age 32 when he was already recognized as a contributor to politics and government. Portrait by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Willson_Peale" title="Charles Willson Peale">Charles Willson Peale</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>After returning to Montpelier in 1774, Madison took a seat on the local <a href="/wiki/Committee_of_safety_(American_Revolution)" title="Committee of safety (American Revolution)">Committee of Safety</a>, a pro-revolution group that oversaw the local Patriot militia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201722_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201722-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In October 1775, he was commissioned as the colonel of the <a href="/wiki/Orange_County,_Virginia" title="Orange County, Virginia">Orange County</a> militia, serving as his father's second-in-command until he was elected as a delegate to the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Virginia_Convention" title="Fifth Virginia Convention">Fifth Virginia Convention</a>, which was charged with producing Virginia's first <a href="/wiki/Constitution" title="Constitution">constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200212–13_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200212–13-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Although Madison never battled in the Revolutionary War, he did rise to prominence in Virginia politics as a wartime leader.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201014–15_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201014–15-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> At the Virginia constitutional convention, he convinced delegates to alter the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Declaration_of_Rights" title="Virginia Declaration of Rights">Virginia Declaration of Rights</a> originally drafted on May 20, 1776, to provide for "equal entitlement", rather than mere "tolerance", in the exercise of religion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201725–27_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201725–27-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> With the enactment of the Virginia constitution, Madison became part of the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_House_of_Delegates" title="Virginia House of Delegates">Virginia House of Delegates</a>, and he was subsequently elected to the Virginia governor's Council of State,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201048–49,_59–60_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201048–49,_59–60-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> where he became a close ally of Governor <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201065–66_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201065–66-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> On July 4, 1776, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence" title="United States Declaration of Independence">United States Declaration of Independence</a> was formally printed, declaring the 13 American states an independent nation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress506–507_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress506–507-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoelho201361_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoelho201361-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison participated in the debates concerning the <a href="/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation" title="Articles of Confederation">Articles of Confederation</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor2016337–339_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2016337–339-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> in November 1777, contributing to the discussion of religious freedom affecting the drafting of the Articles, though his signature was not required for adopting the Articles of Confederation. Madison had proposed liberalizing the article on religious freedom, but the larger Virginia Convention stripped the proposed constitution of the more radical language of "free expression" of faith to the less controversial mention of highlighting "tolerance" within religion. Other amendments by the committee and the entire Convention included the addition of a section on the right to a uniform government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMason1970274–289_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason1970274–289-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Madison again served on the Council of State, from 1777 to 1779, when he was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress" title="Second Continental Congress">Second Continental Congress</a>, the governing body of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During Madison's term in Congress from 1780 to 1783, the U.S. faced a difficult war against Great Britain, as well as runaway <a href="/wiki/Inflation" title="Inflation">inflation</a>, financial troubles, and a lack of cooperation between the different levels of government. According to historian J. C. A. Stagg, Madison worked to become an expert on financial issues, becoming a legislative workhorse and a master of parliamentary coalition building.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStagg,_2019_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStagg,_2019-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Frustrated by the failure of the states to supply needed requisitions, Madison proposed to amend the Articles of Confederation to grant Congress the power to independently raise revenue through <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariffs</a> on imports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201096–97_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201096–97-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>  Though General <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a>, Congressman <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Alexander Hamilton</a>, and other leaders also favored the tariff amendment, it was defeated because it failed to win the ratification of all thirteen states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201096–98_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201096–98-53">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> While a member of Congress, Madison was an ardent supporter of a close alliance between the United States and France. As an advocate of westward expansion, he insisted that the new nation had to ensure its right to navigation on the <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_River" title="Mississippi River">Mississippi River</a> and control of all lands east of it in the Treaty of Paris, which ended the Revolutionary War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003120–123_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003120–123-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Following his term in Congress, Madison won election to the Virginia House of Delegates in 1784.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010xxiv_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010xxiv-55">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ratification_of_the_Constitution">Ratification of the Constitution</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_as_Father_of_the_Constitution" title="James Madison as Father of the Constitution">James Madison as Father of the Constitution</a> and <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(United_States)" title="Constitutional Convention (United States)">Constitutional Convention (United States)</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Confederation_period" title="Confederation period">Confederation period</a></div>
<p>As a member of the Virginia House of Delegates, Madison continued to advocate for religious freedom, and, along with Jefferson, drafted the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Statute_for_Religious_Freedom" title="Virginia Statute for Religious Freedom">Virginia Statute for Religious Freedom</a>. That amendment, which guaranteed freedom of religion and disestablished the Church of England, was passed in 1786.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200217–19_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200217–19-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Madison also became a land speculator, purchasing land along the <a href="/wiki/Mohawk_River" title="Mohawk River">Mohawk River</a> in partnership with another Jefferson protégé, <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">James Monroe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201770_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201770-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the 1780s, Madison became increasingly worried about the disunity of the states and the weakness of the central government after the end of the Revolutionary War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201096–97,_128–130_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201096–97,_128–130-58">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> He believed that <a href="/wiki/Direct_democracy" title="Direct democracy">direct democracy</a> caused social decay and that a <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States" title="Republicanism in the United States">Republican</a> government would be effective against <a href="/wiki/Partisan_(politics)" title="Partisan (politics)">partisanship</a> and factionalism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllott2003321_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllott2003321-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEManweller200522_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEManweller200522-60">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGustafson1992290_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGustafson1992290-61">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> He was particularly troubled by laws that legalized <a href="/wiki/Paper_money" class="mw-redirect" title="Paper money">paper money</a> and denied <a href="/wiki/Diplomatic_immunity" title="Diplomatic immunity">diplomatic immunity</a> to ambassadors from other countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2011104_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2011104-62">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Madison was also concerned about the lack of ability in Congress to capably create foreign policy, protect American trade, and foster the settlement of the lands between the <a href="/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains" title="Appalachian Mountains">Appalachian Mountains</a> and the Mississippi River.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010129–130_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010129–130-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> As Madison wrote, "a crisis had arrived which was to decide whether the American experiment was to be a blessing to the world, or to blast for ever the hopes which the republican cause had inspired."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland198714_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland198714-64">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Madison committed to an intense study of law and political theory and also was influenced by Enlightenment texts sent by Jefferson from France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010136–137_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010136–137-65">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Madison especially sought out works on international law and the constitutions of "ancient and modern confederacies" such as the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Confederation" class="mw-redirect" title="Swiss Confederation">Swiss Confederation</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Achaean_League" title="Achaean League">Achaean League</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201756–57,_74–75_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201756–57,_74–75-66">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> He came to believe that the United States could improve upon past republican experiments by its size which geographically combined 13 colonies; with so many distinct interests competing against each other, Madison hoped to minimize the abuses of <a href="/wiki/Majority_rule" title="Majority rule">majority rule</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201798–99,_121–122_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201798–99,_121–122-67">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, navigation rights to the major trade routes accessed by the Mississippi River highly concerned Madison. He opposed the proposal by <a href="/wiki/John_Jay" title="John Jay">John Jay</a> that the United States concede claims to the river for 25 years, and, according to historian <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Ketcham" title="Ralph Ketcham">Ralph Ketcham</a>, Madison's desire to fight the proposal was a major motivation in his to return to Congress in 1787.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003177–179_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003177–179-68">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Constitution_of_the_United_States,_page_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Image of handwritten copy of the Constitution." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Constitution_of_the_United_States%2C_page_1.jpg/220px-Constitution_of_the_United_States%2C_page_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Constitution_of_the_United_States%2C_page_1.jpg/330px-Constitution_of_the_United_States%2C_page_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Constitution_of_the_United_States%2C_page_1.jpg/440px-Constitution_of_the_United_States%2C_page_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="7258" data-file-height="8785" /></a><figcaption>First page of the original handwritten copy of the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Constitution">U.S. Constitution</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Supportive image of signing of the Constitution with various signers." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg/220px-Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg/330px-Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg/440px-Foundation_of_the_American_Government_by_Henry_Hintermeister.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1312" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a> witnesses <a href="/wiki/Gouverneur_Morris" title="Gouverneur Morris">Gouverneur Morris</a> sign the Constitution while Madison sits in front of <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin" title="Benjamin Franklin">Benjamin Franklin</a> and next to <a href="/wiki/Robert_Morris_(financier)" title="Robert Morris (financier)">Robert Morris</a> in <a href="/wiki/John_Henry_Hintermeister" title="John Henry Hintermeister">John Henry Hintermeister</a>'s  1925 painting, <i>Foundation of the American Government</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERobinson1999117_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobinson1999117-69">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Leading up to the 1787 ratification debates for the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Constitution">Constitution</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017107_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017107-70">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Madison worked with other members of the Virginia delegation, especially <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Randolph" title="Edmund Randolph">Edmund Randolph</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_Mason" title="George Mason">George Mason</a>, to create and present the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Plan" title="Virginia Plan">Virginia Plan</a>, an outline for a new federal constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010150–151_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010150–151-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> It called for three branches of government (legislative, executive, and judicial), a bicameral Congress (consisting of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">Senate</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a>) apportioned by population, and a federal <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Revision" title="Council of Revision">Council of Revision</a> that would have the right to veto laws passed by Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010140–141_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010140–141-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The Virginia Plan did not explicitly lay out the structure of the executive branch, but Madison himself favored a strong <a href="/wiki/Unitary_executive_theory" title="Unitary executive theory">single executive</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017115–117_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017115–117-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Many delegates were surprised to learn that the plan called for the abrogation of the Articles and the creation of a new constitution, to be ratified by special conventions in each state, rather than by the state legislatures. With the assent of prominent attendees such as Washington and <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin" title="Benjamin Franklin">Benjamin Franklin</a>, the delegates agreed in a secret session that the abrogation of the Articles and the creation of a new constitution was a plausible option and began scheduling the process of debating its ratification in the individual states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200225–27_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200225–27-74">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> As a compromise between small and large states, large states got a proportional House, while the small states got equal representation in the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017199–200_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017199–200-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the Philadelphia Convention ended in September 1787, Madison convinced his fellow congressmen to remain neutral in the ratification debate and allow each state to vote on the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010164–166_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010164–166-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Those who supported the Constitution were called Federalists, that included Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021118_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021118-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the United States, opponents of the Constitution, known as <a href="/wiki/Anti-Federalism" title="Anti-Federalism">Anti-Federalists</a>, began a public campaign against ratification.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021118_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021118-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> In response, starting in October 1787,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Federalist_Papersxxv_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Federalist_Papersxxv-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Hamilton and <a href="/wiki/John_Jay" title="John Jay">John Jay</a>, both Federalists, began publishing a series of pro-ratification newspaper articles in New York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017177–178_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017177–178-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> After Jay dropped out of the project, Hamilton approached Madison, who was in New York on congressional business, to write some of the essays.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017179–180_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017179–180-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The essays were published under the <a href="/wiki/Pseudonym" title="Pseudonym">pseudonym</a> of Publius.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021130_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021130-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamiltonMadisonJay1992xxv_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamiltonMadisonJay1992xxv-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The trio produced 85 essays known as <i><a href="/wiki/The_Federalist_Papers" title="The Federalist Papers">The Federalist Papers</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamiltonMadisonJay1992xxv_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamiltonMadisonJay1992xxv-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The 85 essays were divided into two parts, 36 letters were against the Articles of Confederation, and 49 letters that favored the new Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Federalist_Papersxxv_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Federalist_Papersxxv-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The articles were also published in book form and used by the supporters of the Constitution in the ratifying conventions. <a href="/wiki/Federalist_No._10" title="Federalist No. 10">Federalist No. 10</a>, Madison's first contribution to <i>The Federalist Papers</i>, became highly regarded in the 20th century for its advocacy of <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democracy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200231–35_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200231–35-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In it, Madison describes the dangers posed by the majority <a href="/wiki/Political_faction" title="Political faction">factions</a> and argues that their effects can be limited through the formation of a large republic. He theorizes that in large republics the large number of factions that emerge will control their influence because no single faction can become a majority.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamiltonMadisonJay1992_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamiltonMadisonJay1992-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017180–183_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017180–183-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Federalist_No._51" title="Federalist No. 51">Federalist No. 51</a>, he goes on to explain how the separation of powers between three branches of the federal government, as well as between state governments and the federal government, establishes a system of <a href="/wiki/Checks_and_balances" class="mw-redirect" title="Checks and balances">checks and balances</a> that ensures that no one institution would become too powerful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017208–209_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017208–209-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>As the Virginia ratification convention began, Madison focused his efforts on winning the support of the relatively small number of undecided delegates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010179–180_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010179–180-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> His long correspondence with Randolph paid off at the convention, as Randolph announced that he would support unconditional ratification of the Constitution, with amendments to be proposed after ratification.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017231–233_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017231–233-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Though former Virginia governor <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Henry" title="Patrick Henry">Patrick Henry</a> gave several persuasive speeches arguing against ratification, Madison's expertise on the subject he had long argued for allowed him to respond with rational arguments to Henry's <a href="/wiki/Anti-federalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-federalism">anti-Federalist</a> appeals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200235–37_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200235–37-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Madison was also a defender of federal veto rights and, according to historian Ron Chernow "pleaded at the Constitutional Convention that the federal government should possess a veto over state laws".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChernow2004571–74_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernow2004571–74-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> In his final speech to the ratifying convention, Madison implored his fellow delegates to ratify the Constitution as it had been written, arguing that failure to do so would lead to the collapse of the entire ratification effort, as each state would seek favorable amendments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017239–240_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017239–240-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> On June 25, 1788, the convention voted 89–79 in favor of ratification. The vote came a week after New Hampshire became the ninth state to ratify, thereby securing the Constitution's adoption and with that, a new form of government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmar20056_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmar20056-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> The following January, Washington was elected the nation's <a href="/wiki/1788-89_United_States_presidential_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1788-89 United States presidential election">first president</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMount_Vernon,_Essay_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMount_Vernon,_Essay-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Congressman_and_party_leader_.281789.E2.80.931801.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Congressman_and_party_leader_(1789–1801)">Congressman and party leader (1789–1801)</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_to_Congress">Election to Congress</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_George_Washington" title="Presidency of George Washington">Presidency of George Washington</a></div>
<p>After Virginia ratified the constitution, Madison returned to New York and resumed his duties in the Congress of the Confederation. After Madison was defeated in his bid for the Senate, and with concerns for both his political career and the possibility that <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Henry" title="Patrick Henry">Patrick Henry</a> and his allies would arrange for a second constitutional convention, Madison ran for the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200238–39_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200238–39-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017247–248,_251–252_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017247–248,_251–252-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Henry and the Anti-Federalists were in firm control of the General Assembly in the autumn of 1788.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> At Henry's behest, the Virginia legislature designed to deny Madison a seat, and created congressional districts.  Henry and his supporters ensured that Orange County was in a district heavily populated with Anti-Federalists, roughly three to one, to oppose Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online_96-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021162_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021162-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> This practice is called <i><a href="/wiki/Gerrymandering_in_the_United_States" title="Gerrymandering in the United States">gerrymandering</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online_96-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_Founders_Online-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Henry also recruited <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">James Monroe</a>, a strong challenger to Madison. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021162_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021162-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Locked in a difficult race against Monroe, Madison promised to support a series of constitutional amendments to protect individual liberties.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200238–39_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200238–39-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In an open letter, Madison wrote that, while he had opposed requiring alterations to the Constitution before ratification, he now believed that "amendments, if pursued with a proper moderation and in a proper mode&#160;... may serve the double purpose of satisfying the minds of well-meaning opponents, and of providing additional guards in favor of liberty."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017252–253_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017252–253-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Madison's promise paid off, as in <a href="/wiki/1789_Virginia%27s_5th_congressional_district_election" title="1789 Virginia&#39;s 5th congressional district election">Virginia's 5th district election</a>, he gained a seat in Congress with 57 percent of the vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELabunski2006148–150_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELabunski2006148–150-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison became a key adviser to Washington, who valued Madison's understanding of the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200238–39_94-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200238–39-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Madison helped Washington write his <a href="/wiki/First_inauguration_of_George_Washington" title="First inauguration of George Washington">first inaugural address</a> and also prepared the official House response to Washington's speech. He played a significant role in establishing and staffing the three <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_States" title="Cabinet of the United States">Cabinet</a> departments, and his influence helped Thomas Jefferson become the first <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010189–193,_203_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010189–193,_203-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> At the start of the <a href="/wiki/1st_United_States_Congress" title="1st United States Congress">first Congress</a>, he introduced a tariff bill similar to the one he had advocated for under the Articles of the Confederation,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017258–259_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017258–259-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> and Congress established a federal tariff on imports by enacting the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1789" title="Tariff of 1789">Tariff of 1789</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBordewich2016100–102_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBordewich2016100–102-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The following year, Secretary of the Treasury Hamilton introduced an ambitious economic program that called for the federal assumption of state debts and the funding of that debt through the issuance of federal <a href="/wiki/Security_(finance)" title="Security (finance)">securities</a>. Hamilton's plan favored <a href="/wiki/Northern_United_States" title="Northern United States">Northern</a> speculators and was disadvantageous to states, such as Virginia, that had already paid off most of their debt; Madison emerged as one of the principal Congressional opponents of the plan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010213–217_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010213–217-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> After prolonged legislative deadlock, Madison, Jefferson, and Hamilton agreed to the <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1790" title="Compromise of 1790">Compromise of 1790</a>, which provided for the enactment of Hamilton's assumption plan, as part of the <a href="/wiki/Funding_Act_of_1790" title="Funding Act of 1790">Funding Act of 1790</a>. In return, Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/Residence_Act" title="Residence Act">Residence Act</a>, which established the federal capital district of <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, on the Potomac River.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010217–220_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010217–220-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bill_of_Rights">Bill of Rights</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights" title="United States Bill of Rights">United States Bill of Rights</a></div>
<p>During the first Congress, Madison took the lead in advocating for several constitutional amendments to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights" title="United States Bill of Rights">Bill of Rights</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010195–197_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010195–197-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> His primary goals were to fulfill his 1789 campaign pledge and to prevent the calling of a second constitutional convention, but he also hoped to safeguard the rights and liberties of the people against broad actions of Congress and individual states. He believed that the enumeration of specific rights would fix those rights in the public mind and encourage judges to protect them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017264–274_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017264–274-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990290_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990290-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> After studying more than two-hundred amendments that had been proposed at the state ratifying conventions,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010197–199_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010197–199-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Madison introduced the Bill of Rights on June 8, 1789. His amendments contained numerous restrictions on the federal government and would protect, among other things, freedom of religion, freedom of speech, and the right to peaceful assembly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017267–269_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017267–269-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> While most of his proposed amendments were drawn from the ratifying conventions, Madison was largely responsible for proposals to guarantee <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press" title="Freedom of the press">freedom of the press</a>, protect property from government seizure, and ensure <a href="/wiki/Jury_trial" title="Jury trial">jury trials</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010197–199_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010197–199-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> He also proposed an amendment to prevent states from abridging "equal rights of conscience, or freedom of the press, or the trial by jury in criminal cases".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017270–271_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017270–271-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison's Bill of Rights faced little opposition; he had largely co-opted the Anti-Federalist goal of amending the Constitution but had avoided proposing amendments that would alienate supporters of the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017274–275_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017274–275-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> His amendments were mostly adopted by the House of Representatives as proposed, but the Senate made several changes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017275–276_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017275–276-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Madison's proposal to apply parts of the Bill of Rights to the states was eliminated, as was his change to the Constitution's preamble which he thought would be enhanced by including a prefatory paragraph indicating that governmental power is vested by the people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELabunski2006232_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELabunski2006232-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> He was disappointed that the Bill of Rights did not include protections against actions by state governments,<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> but the passage of the document mollified some critics of the original constitution and shored up his support in Virginia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010197–199_107-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010197–199-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Ten amendments were finally ratified on December 15, 1791, becoming known in their final form as the Bill of Rights.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBill_of_Rights,_National_Archives_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBill_of_Rights,_National_Archives-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Founding_the_Democratic.E2.80.93Republican_Party"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Founding_the_Democratic–Republican_Party">Founding the Democratic–Republican Party</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_(by_Rembrandt_Peale,_1800).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Image of Jefferson who was a close friend and confidant of Madison." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_%28by_Rembrandt_Peale%2C_1800%29.jpg/220px-Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_%28by_Rembrandt_Peale%2C_1800%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_%28by_Rembrandt_Peale%2C_1800%29.jpg/330px-Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_%28by_Rembrandt_Peale%2C_1800%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_%28by_Rembrandt_Peale%2C_1800%29.jpg/440px-Official_Presidential_portrait_of_Thomas_Jefferson_%28by_Rembrandt_Peale%2C_1800%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2512" data-file-height="2996" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> founded the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a> with Madison and broadly represented Southern interests.</figcaption></figure>
<p>After 1790, the Washington administration became polarized into two main factions. One faction, led by Jefferson and Madison, broadly represented Southern interests and sought close relations with France. This faction became the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a> opposition to Secretary of the Treasury Hamilton. The other faction, led by Hamilton and the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_party" class="mw-redirect" title="Federalist party">Federalists</a>, broadly represented Northern financial interests and favored close relations with Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010207–208_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010207–208-118">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In 1791, Hamilton introduced a plan that called for the establishment of a <a href="/wiki/History_of_central_banking_in_the_United_States" title="History of central banking in the United States">national bank</a> to provide loans to emerging industries and oversee the money supply.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010221–224_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010221–224-119">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Madison and the Democratic-Republican Party fought back against Hamilton's attempt to expand the power of the Federal Government with the formation of a national bank. Therefore, they opposed Hamilton's plan and Madison argued that under the Constitution, Congress did not have the power to create a federally empowered national bank.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017199–211_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017199–211-120">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Despite Madison's opposition, Congress passed a bill to create the <a href="/wiki/First_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="First Bank of the United States">First Bank of the United States</a>, which Washington signed into law in February 1791.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010221–224_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010221–224-119">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> As Hamilton implemented his economic program and Washington continued to enjoy immense prestige as president, Madison became increasingly concerned that Hamilton would seek to abolish the federal republic in favor of a centralized monarchy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017343_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017343-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Hamilton submitted his <i><a href="/wiki/Report_on_Manufactures" title="Report on Manufactures">Report on Manufactures</a></i>, which called for federal action to stimulate the development of a diversified economy, Madison once again challenged Hamilton's proposal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017343–347_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017343–347-122">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Along with Jefferson, Madison helped <a href="/wiki/Philip_Freneau" title="Philip Freneau">Philip Freneau</a> establish the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Gazette" title="National Gazette">National Gazette</a></i>, a Philadelphia newspaper that attacked Hamilton's proposals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017324–326_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017324–326-123">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> In an essay published in the newspaper in September 1792, Madison wrote that the country had divided into two factions: his faction, which believed "that mankind are capable of governing themselves", and Hamilton's faction, which allegedly sought the establishment of an aristocratic monarchy and was biased in favor of the wealthy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017366–367_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017366–367-124">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Those opposed to Hamilton's economic policies, including many former Anti-Federalists, continued to strengthen the ranks of the Democratic–Republican Party,<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> while those who supported the administration's policies supported Hamilton's Federalist Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010279–280_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010279–280-127">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1792_United_States_presidential_election" title="1792 United States presidential election">1792 presidential election</a>, both major parties supported Washington for re-election, but the Democratic–Republicans sought to unseat Vice President <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John Adams</a>. Because the Constitution's rules essentially precluded Jefferson from challenging Adams,<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> the party backed New York Governor <a href="/wiki/George_Clinton_(vice_president)" title="George Clinton (vice president)">George Clinton</a> for the vice presidency, but Adams won nonetheless.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017369–370_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017369–370-130">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Jefferson out of office after 1793, Madison became the de facto leader of the Democratic–Republican Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010305–306_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010305–306-131">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> When Britain and France <a href="/wiki/French_Revolutionary_Wars" title="French Revolutionary Wars">went to war</a> in 1793, the U.S. needed to determine which side to support.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010261–262_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010261–262-132">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> While the differences between the Democratic–Republicans and the Federalists had previously centered on economic matters, foreign policy became an increasingly important issue, as Madison and Jefferson favored France and Hamilton favored Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017373–374_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017373–374-133">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> War with Britain became imminent in 1794 after the British seized hundreds of American ships that were trading with French colonies. Madison believed that a trade war with Britain would probably succeed, and would allow Americans to assert their independence fully. The <a href="/wiki/British_West_Indies" title="British West Indies">British West Indies</a>, Madison maintained, could not live without American foodstuffs, but Americans could easily do without British manufacturers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVarg196374_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVarg196374-134">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Washington then secured friendly trade relations with Britain through the <a href="/wiki/Jay_Treaty" title="Jay Treaty">Jay Treaty</a> of 1794.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200238–44_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200238–44-135">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Madison and his Democratic–Republican allies were outraged by the treaty; the Democratic–Republican <a href="/wiki/Robert_R._Livingston_(chancellor)" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert R. Livingston (chancellor)">Robert R. Livingston</a> wrote to Madison that the treaty "sacrifices every essential interest and prostrates the honor of our country".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017396–398_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017396–398-136">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> Madison's strong opposition to the treaty led to a permanent break with Washington, ending their friendship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200238–44_135-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200238–44-135">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_family">Marriage and family</span></h3>
<p>On September 15, 1794, Madison married <a href="/wiki/Dolley_Payne_Todd" class="mw-redirect" title="Dolley Payne Todd">Dolley Payne Todd</a>, the 26-year-old widow of John Todd, a Quaker farmer who died during a <a href="/wiki/Yellow_fever" title="Yellow fever">yellow fever</a> epidemic in Philadelphia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990377_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990377-137">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Earlier that year, Madison and Dolley Todd had been formally introduced at Madison's request by Aaron Burr. Burr had become friends with her when staying at the same Philadelphia boardinghouse.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrant1950406–407_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrant1950406–407-138">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> After an arranged meeting in early 1794, the two quickly became romantically engaged and prepared for a wedding that summer, but Todd suffered recurring illnesses because of her exposure to yellow fever in Philadelphia. They eventually traveled to <a href="/wiki/Harewood_(West_Virginia)" title="Harewood (West Virginia)">Harewood in Virginia</a> for their wedding. Only a few close family members attended, and <a href="/wiki/Winchester,_Virginia" title="Winchester, Virginia">Winchester</a> reverend <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Balmain" title="Alexander Balmain">Alexander Balmain</a> presided.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003376–380_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003376–380-139">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Dolley became a renowned figure in Washington, D.C., and excelled at hosting dinners and other important political occasions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>  She subsequently helped to establish the modern image of the first lady of the United States as an individual who has a leading role in the social affairs of the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017479–480_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017479–480-140">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
