</p><p>Throughout his life, Madison maintained a close relationship with his father, James Sr. Eventually at age 50, Madison inherited the large plantation of Montpelier and other possessions, including his father's numerous slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017443–444_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017443–444-141">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003370–371_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003370–371-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> While Madison never had children with Dolley, he adopted her one surviving son, <a href="/wiki/John_Payne_Todd" title="John Payne Todd">John Payne Todd</a> (known as Payne), after the couple's marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010321–322_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010321–322-142">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Some of his colleagues, such as Monroe and Burr, believed Madison's lack of offspring weighed on his thoughts, though he never spoke of any distress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003386_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003386-143">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, <a href="/wiki/Oral_history" title="Oral history">oral history</a> has suggested Madison may have fathered a child with his enslaved half-sister, a cook named Coreen, but researchers were unable to gather the <a href="/wiki/DNA_evidence" class="mw-redirect" title="DNA evidence">DNA evidence</a> needed to determine the validity of the accusation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMummolo2007_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMummolo2007-144">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENelson201619–20_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENelson201619–20-145">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Adams_presidency">Adams presidency</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_John_Adams" title="Presidency of John Adams">Presidency of John Adams</a></div>
<p>Washington chose to retire after serving two terms and, in advance of the <a href="/wiki/1796_United_States_presidential_election" title="1796 United States presidential election">1796 presidential election</a>, Madison helped convince Jefferson to run for the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010305–306_131-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010305–306-131">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Despite Madison's efforts, Federalist candidate John Adams defeated Jefferson, taking a narrow majority of the electoral vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010317–318_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010317–318-146">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Under the rules of the Electoral College then in place, Jefferson became vice president because he finished with the second-most electoral votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017408–400_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017408–400-147">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Madison, meanwhile, had declined to seek re-election to the House, and he returned to Montpelier.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010321–322_142-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010321–322-142">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> On Jefferson's advice, Adams considered appointing Madison to an American delegation charged with ending French attacks on American shipping, but Adams's cabinet members strongly opposed the idea.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017411–414_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017411–414-148">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though he was out of office, Madison remained a prominent Democratic–Republican leader in opposition to the Adams administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200248–49_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200248–49-149">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChernow2004571–574_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernow2004571–574-150">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Madison and Jefferson believed that the Federalists were using the <a href="/wiki/Quasi-War" title="Quasi-War">Quasi-War</a> with France to justify the violation of constitutional rights by passing the <a href="/wiki/Alien_and_Sedition_Acts" title="Alien and Sedition Acts">Alien and Sedition Acts</a>, and they increasingly came to view Adams as a monarchist.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017415–417_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017415–417-151">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Both Madison and Jefferson, as leaders of the Democratic–Republican Party, expressed the belief that natural rights were non-negotiable even during a time of war. Madison believed that the Alien and Sedition Acts formed a dangerous precedent, by giving the government the power to look past the natural rights of its people in the name of national security.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETime_Magazine,_July_5,_2004_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETime_Magazine,_July_5,_2004-153">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> In response to the Alien and Sedition Acts, Jefferson argued that the states had the power to <a href="/wiki/Nullification_(U.S._Constitution)" title="Nullification (U.S. Constitution)">nullify</a> federal law on the basis of the Constitution was a compact among the states. Madison rejected this view of nullification and urged that states respond to unjust federal laws through <a href="/wiki/Interposition" title="Interposition">interposition</a>, a process by which a state legislature declared a law to be unconstitutional but did not take steps to actively prevent its enforcement. Jefferson's doctrine of nullification was widely rejected, and the incident damaged the Democratic–Republican Party as attention was shifted from the Alien and Sedition Acts to the unpopular nullification doctrine.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017417–421_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017417–421-154">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1799, Madison was elected to the Virginia legislature. At the same time, Madison planned for Jefferson's campaign in the <a href="/wiki/1800_United_States_presidential_election" title="1800 United States presidential election">1800 presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017424–425_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017424–425-155">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Madison issued the <a href="/wiki/Report_of_1800" title="Report of 1800">Report of 1800</a>, which attacked the Alien and Sedition Acts as unconstitutional. That report held that Congress was limited to legislating on its <a href="/wiki/Enumerated_powers_(United_States)" title="Enumerated powers (United States)">enumerated powers</a> and that punishment for sedition violated freedom of speech and freedom of the press. Jefferson embraced the report, and it became the unofficial Democratic–Republican platform for the 1800 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017428–430_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017428–430-156">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> With the Federalists divided between supporters of Hamilton and Adams, and with news of the end of the Quasi-War not reaching the United States until after the election, Jefferson and his running mate, <a href="/wiki/Aaron_Burr" title="Aaron Burr">Aaron Burr</a>, defeated Adams, allowing Jefferson to prevail as president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017433–436_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017433–436-157">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017438–439_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017438–439-158">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Secretary_of_State_.281801.E2.80.931809.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_(1801–1809)">Secretary of State (1801–1809)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Thomas_Jefferson" title="Presidency of Thomas Jefferson">Presidency of Thomas Jefferson</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chesapeake%E2%80%93Leopard_affair" title="Chesapeake–Leopard affair">Chesapeake–Leopard affair</a></div>
<p>Madison was one of two major influences in Jefferson's Cabinet, the other being Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/Albert_Gallatin" title="Albert Gallatin">Albert Gallatin</a>. Madison was appointed secretary of state despite lacking foreign policy experience.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200250–51_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200250–51-159">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcDonald197636–38_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDonald197636–38-160">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> An introspective individual, he received assistance from his wife,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017479–480_140-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017479–480-140">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> relying deeply on her in dealing with the social pressures of being a public figure both in his own Cabinet appointment as secretary of state and afterward.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> As the ascent of <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a> in France had dulled Democratic–Republican enthusiasm for the French cause, Madison sought a neutral position in the ongoing <a href="/wiki/Coalition_Wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Coalition Wars">Coalition Wars</a> between France and Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010373–374_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010373–374-161">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Domestically, the Jefferson administration and the Democratic–Republican Congress rolled back many Federalist policies; Congress quickly repealed the Alien and Sedition Act, abolished internal taxes, and reduced the size of the army and navy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcDonald197642–44_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDonald197642–44-162">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> Gallatin, however, did convince Jefferson to retain the First Bank of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009293–296_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009293–296-163">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Though the Federalist political power was rapidly fading away at the national level, Chief Justice <a href="/wiki/John_Marshall" title="John Marshall">John Marshall</a> ensured that Federalist ideology retained an important presence in the judiciary. In the case of <i><a href="/wiki/Marbury_v._Madison" title="Marbury v. Madison">Marbury v. Madison</a></i>, Marshall simultaneously ruled that Madison had unjustly refused to deliver federal commissions to individuals who had been appointed by the previous administration, but that the Supreme Court did not have jurisdiction over the case. Most importantly, Marshall's opinion established the principle of <a href="/wiki/Judicial_review" title="Judicial review">judicial review</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017465–466_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017465–466-164">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> While attaining the position of secretary of state and throughout his life, Madison maintained contact with his father, James Sr., who died in 1801 and which allowed Madison to inherit the large plantation of Montpelier.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017443–444_141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017443–444-141">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louisiana_Purchase.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Map of Louisiana Purchase under Jefferson as supported by Madison." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Louisiana_Purchase.png/330px-Louisiana_Purchase.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Louisiana_Purchase.png/495px-Louisiana_Purchase.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Louisiana_Purchase.png/660px-Louisiana_Purchase.png 2x" data-file-width="3433" data-file-height="2414" /></a><figcaption>The 1803 Louisiana Purchase totaled 827,987 <a href="/wiki/Square_mile" title="Square mile">square miles</a> (2,144,480 square kilometers), doubling the size of the United States.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jefferson took office and was sympathetic to the westward expansion of Americans who had settled as far west as the Mississippi River; his sympathy for expansion was supported by his concern for the sparse regional demographics in the far west compared to the more populated eastern states, the far west being inhabited almost exclusively by <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Native Americans</a>. Jefferson promoted such western expansion and hoped to acquire the <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spanish</a> territory of <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_(New_France)" title="Louisiana (New France)">Louisiana</a>, west of the Mississippi River, for expansionist purposes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009357–359,_366–367_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009357–359,_366–367-165">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Early in Jefferson's presidency, the administration learned that Spain planned to retrocede the Louisiana territory to France, raising fears of French encroachment on U.S. territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010374–376_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010374–376-166">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> In 1802, Jefferson and Madison sent Monroe, a sympathetic fellow Virginian, to France to negotiate the purchase of <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>, which controlled access to the Mississippi River and thus was immensely important to the farmers of the American frontier. Rather than merely selling New Orleans, Napoleon's government, having already given up on plans to establish a new French empire in the Americas, offered to sell the entire territory of Louisiana. Despite lacking explicit authorization from Jefferson, Monroe, along with Livingston, whom Jefferson had appointed as America's minister to France, negotiated the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>, in which France sold more than 
827,987 square miles (2,144,480 square kilometers) of land in exchange for $15 million (equivalent to $271,433,333.33&#32;in 2021). <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010382–389_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010382–389-167">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Portrait of Madison by Gilbert Stuart" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg/220px-James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="271" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg/330px-James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg/440px-James_Madison_by_Gilbert_Stuart.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2228" data-file-height="2742" /></a><figcaption>James Madison as Secretary of State painted by <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Stuart" title="Gilbert Stuart">Gilbert Stuart</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1805–1807</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite the time-sensitive nature of negotiations with the French, Jefferson was concerned about the constitutionality of the Louisiana Purchase, and he privately favored introducing a constitutional amendment explicitly authorizing Congress to acquire new territories. Madison convinced Jefferson to refrain from proposing the amendment, and the administration ultimately submitted the Louisiana Purchase Treaty for approval by the Senate, without an accompanying constitutional amendment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017463–465_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017463–465-168">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Unlike Jefferson, Madison was not seriously concerned with the constitutionality of the purchase. He believed that the circumstances did not warrant a strict interpretation of the Constitution, because the expansion was in the country's best interest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003422_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham2003422-169">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> The Senate quickly ratified the treaty, and the House, with equal alacrity, passed enabling legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990419–421_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990419–421-170">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200251–52_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200251–52-171">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017462–463_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017462–463-172">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Early in his tenure, Jefferson was able to maintain cordial relations with both France and Britain, but relations with Britain deteriorated after 1805.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcDonald1976100–101_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDonald1976100–101-173">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> The British ended their policy of tolerance towards American shipping and began seizing American goods headed for French ports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009640–642_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009640–642-174">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> They also <a href="/wiki/Impressment" title="Impressment">impressed</a> American sailors, some of whom had originally defected from the British navy, but some of whom had never been British subjects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200281–84_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200281–84-175">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> In response to the attacks, Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/Non-importation_Act" title="Non-importation Act">Non-importation Act</a>, which restricted many, but not all, British imports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009640–642_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009640–642-174">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Tensions with Britain were heightened due to the <a href="/wiki/Chesapeake%E2%80%93Leopard_affair" title="Chesapeake–Leopard affair"><i>Chesapeake–Leopard</i> affair</a>, a June 1807 naval confrontation between American and British naval forces, while the French also began attacking American shipping.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009644–649_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009644–649-176">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Madison believed that economic pressure could force the British to end their seizure of American shipped goods, and he and Jefferson convinced Congress to pass the <a href="/wiki/Embargo_Act_of_1807" title="Embargo Act of 1807">Embargo Act of 1807</a>, which banned all exports to foreign nations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017493–495_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017493–495-177">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> The embargo proved ineffective, unpopular, and difficult to enforce, especially in New England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017494–495_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017494–495-178">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> In March 1809, Congress replaced the embargo with the <a href="/wiki/Non-Intercourse_Act_(1809)" title="Non-Intercourse Act (1809)">Non-Intercourse Act</a>, which allowed trade with nations other than Britain and France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009652–657_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009652–657-179">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1808_presidential_election">1808 presidential election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1808_United_States_presidential_election" title="1808 United States presidential election">1808 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1808.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Election maps by state for election of 1808." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/ElectoralCollege1808.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1808.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/ElectoralCollege1808.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1808.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/ElectoralCollege1808.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1808.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="577" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>Following Jefferson's presidency, Madison's 1808 electoral vote results</figcaption></figure>
<p>Speculation regarding Madison's potential succession to Jefferson commenced early in Jefferson's first term. Madison's status in the party was damaged by his association with the embargo, which was unpopular throughout the country and especially in the Northeast.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010457–458_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010457–458-180">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> With the Federalists collapsing as a national party after 1800, the chief opposition to Madison's candidacy came from other members of the Democratic–Republican Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010438–439_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010438–439-181">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Madison became the target of attacks from Congressman <a href="/wiki/John_Randolph_of_Roanoke" title="John Randolph of Roanoke">John Randolph</a>, a leader of a faction of the party known as the <a href="/wiki/Tertium_quids" title="Tertium quids">tertium quids</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010434–435_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010434–435-182">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Randolph recruited Monroe, who had felt betrayed by the administration's rejection of the proposed <a href="/wiki/Monroe%E2%80%93Pinkney_Treaty" title="Monroe–Pinkney Treaty">Monroe–Pinkney Treaty</a> with Britain, to challenge Madison for leadership of the party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017496–497_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017496–497-183">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Many Northerners, meanwhile, hoped that Vice President Clinton could unseat Madison as Jefferson's successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010457–459_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010457–459-184">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Despite this opposition, Madison won his party's presidential nomination at the January 1808 <a href="/wiki/Congressional_nominating_caucus" title="Congressional nominating caucus">congressional nominating caucus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017498_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017498-185">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> The Federalist Party mustered little strength outside New England, and Madison easily defeated Federalist candidate <a href="/wiki/Charles_Cotesworth_Pinckney" title="Charles Cotesworth Pinckney">Charles Cotesworth Pinckney</a> in the general election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland19905_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland19905-186">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281809.E2.80.931817.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1809–1817)">Presidency (1809–1817)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Madison" title="Presidency of James Madison">Presidency of James Madison</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Inauguration_and_cabinet">Inauguration and cabinet</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Madison engraving circa 1809." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg/220px-James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="334" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg/330px-James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg/440px-James_Madison_Portrait2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="687" data-file-height="1044" /></a><figcaption>James Madison as President, engraving by <a href="/wiki/David_Edwin" title="David Edwin">David Edwin</a> from between 1809 and 1817</figcaption></figure>
<p>Madison's inauguration took place on March 4, 1809, in the House chamber of the U.S. Capitol. Chief Justice Marshall administered the presidential oath of office to Madison while outgoing President Jefferson watched from a seat close by.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrant195613_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrant195613-187">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> Vice President George Clinton was sworn in for a second term, making him the first U.S. vice president to serve under two presidents. Unlike Jefferson, who enjoyed relatively unified support, Madison faced political opposition from previous political allies such as Monroe and Clinton. Additionally, the Federalist Party was resurgent owing to opposition to the embargo. Aside from his planned nomination of Gallatin for secretary of state, the remaining members of Madison's Cabinet were chosen merely to further political harmony, and, according to historians Ketcham and Rutland, were largely unremarkable or incompetent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199032–33,_51,_55_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199032–33,_51,_55-188">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200261–62_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200261–62-189">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Due to the opposition of Monroe and Clinton, Madison immediately faced opposition to his planned nomination of Secretary of the Treasury Gallatin as secretary of state. Madison eventually chose not to nominate Gallatin, keeping him in the treasury department.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199032–33_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199032–33-190">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison settled instead for <a href="/wiki/Robert_Smith_(Cabinet_member)" title="Robert Smith (Cabinet member)">Robert Smith</a>, the brother of Maryland Senator Samuel Smith, to be the secretary of state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200261–62_189-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200261–62-189">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> However, for the next two years, Madison performed most of the job of the secretary of state due to Smith's incompetence. After bitter intra-party contention, Madison finally replaced Smith with Monroe in April 1811.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200262_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200262-191">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017524–527_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017524–527-192">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> With a Cabinet full of those he distrusted, Madison rarely called Cabinet meetings and instead frequently consulted with Gallatin alone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200264–65_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200264–65-193">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Early in his presidency, Madison sought to continue Jefferson's policies of low taxes and a reduction of the national debt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199020_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199020-194">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> In 1811, Congress allowed the charter of the First Bank of the United States to lapse after Madison declined to take a strong stance on the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199068–70_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199068–70-195">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_of_1812">War of 1812</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Origins_of_the_War_of_1812" title="Origins of the War of 1812">Origins of the War of 1812</a></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Prelude_to_war">Prelude to war</span></h4>
<p>Congress had repealed the Embargo Act of 1807 shortly before Madison became president, but troubles with the British and French continued.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199013_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199013-196">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Madison settled on a new strategy that was designed to pit the British and French against each other, offering to trade with whichever country would end their attacks against American shipping. The gambit almost succeeded, but negotiations with the British collapsed in mid-1809.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017508–512_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017508–512-197">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> Seeking to drive a wedge between the Americans and the British, Napoleon offered to end French attacks on American shipping so long as the United States punished any countries that did not similarly end restrictions on trade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199062–64_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199062–64-198">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> Madison accepted Napoleon's proposal in the hope that it would convince the British to finally end their policy of commercial warfare. Notwithstanding, the British refused to change their policies, and the French reneged on their promise and continued to attack American shipping.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199064–66,_81_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199064–66,_81-199">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With sanctions and other policies having failed, Madison determined that war with Britain was the only remaining option.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017532–537_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017532–537-200">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Many Americans called for a "second war of independence" to restore honor and stature to their new nation, and an angry public elected a "<a href="/wiki/War_hawk" title="War hawk">war hawk</a>" Congress, led by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERisjord1961196–210_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERisjord1961196–210-201">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> With Britain already engaged in the Napoleonic Wars, many Americans including Madison believed that the United States could easily capture <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a>, using it as a bargaining chip for other disputes or simply retaining control of it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200297–98_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200297–98-202">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> On June 1, 1812, Madison asked Congress for a declaration of war, stating that the United States could no longer tolerate Britain's "state of war against the United States". The declaration of war was passed along sectional and party lines, with opposition to the declaration coming from Federalists and from some Democratic–Republicans in the Northeast.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017543–545_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017543–545-203">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> In the years prior to the war, Jefferson and Madison had reduced the size of the military, leaving the country with a military force consisting mostly of poorly trained militia members.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990159_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990159-204">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> Madison asked Congress to quickly put the country "into an armor and an attitude demanded by the crisis", specifically recommending expansion of the army and navy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990509_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990509-205">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_actions">Military actions</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Naval warfare of USS Constitution in battle." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg/220px-USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="151" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg/330px-USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg/440px-USS_Constitution_vs_Guerriere.jpg 2x" data-file-width="740" data-file-height="509" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/USS_Constitution" title="USS Constitution">USS&#160;<i>Constitution</i></a> defeats <a href="/wiki/HMS_Guerriere_(1806)" title="HMS Guerriere (1806)">HMS <i>Guerriere</i></a>, a significant event during the war. U.S. nautical victories boosted American morale.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Given the circumstances involving Napoleon in Europe Madison initially believed the war would result in a quick American victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200297–98_202-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200297–98-202">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Madison ordered three landed military spearhead incursions into Canada, starting from Fort Detroit, designed to loosen British control around American-held <a href="/wiki/Fort_Niagara" title="Fort Niagara">Fort Niagara</a> and destroy the British supply lines from <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>. These actions would gain leverage for concessions to protect American shipping in the Atlantic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265_206-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Without a standing army, Madison counted on regular state militias to rally to the flag and invade Canada: still, governors in the Northeast failed to cooperate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017551–552_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017551–552-207">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>  The British army was more organized, used professional soldiers, and fostered an alliance with <a href="/wiki/Tecumseh%27s_confederacy" title="Tecumseh&#39;s confederacy">Native American tribes led by Tecumseh</a>. On August 16, during the British <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Detroit" title="Siege of Detroit">siege of Detroit</a>, Major General <a href="/wiki/William_Hull" title="William Hull">William Hull</a> panicked, after the British fired on the fort, killing two American officers. Terrified of an Indian attack, Hull ordered a white tablecloth out a window and unconditionally surrendered Fort Detroit and his entire army to British Major-General Sir <a href="/wiki/Issac_Brock" class="mw-redirect" title="Issac Brock">Issac Brock</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265_206-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Hull was court-martialed for cowardness, but Madison intervened and saved him from being shot. On October 13, a separate force from the United States was defeated at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Queenston_Heights" title="Battle of Queenston Heights">Queenston Heights</a>, although Brock was killed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017548–550_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017548–550-208">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265_206-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Commanding General <a href="/wiki/Henry_Dearborn" title="Henry Dearborn">Henry Dearborn</a>, hampered by mutinous New England infantry, retreated to winter quarters near <a href="/wiki/Albany,_New_York" title="Albany, New York">Albany</a>, failing to destroy Montreal's vulnerable British supply lines.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265_206-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Lacking adequate revenue to fund the war, the Madison administration was forced to rely on high-interest loans furnished by bankers based in New York City and Philadelphia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990126–127_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990126–127-209">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1812_United_States_presidential_election" title="1812 United States presidential election">1812 presidential election</a>, held during the early stages of the war, Madison was re-nominated without opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199092–93_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199092–93-210">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> A dissident group of New York Democratic-Republicans nominated <a href="/wiki/DeWitt_Clinton" title="DeWitt Clinton">DeWitt Clinton</a>, the lieutenant governor of New York and a nephew of recently deceased Vice President George Clinton, to oppose Madison in the 1812 election. This faction of Democratic-Republicans hoped to unseat the president by forging a coalition among Democratic-Republicans opposed to the coming war, as well as those party faithful angry with Madison for not moving more decisively toward war, northerners weary of the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_dynasty" title="Virginia dynasty">Virginia dynasty</a> and southern control of the White House, and many <a href="/wiki/New_England" title="New England">New Englanders</a> wanted Madison replaced. Dismayed about their prospects of beating Madison, a group of top Federalists met with Clinton's supporters to discuss a <a href="/wiki/Electoral_alliance" title="Electoral alliance">unification strategy</a>. Difficult as it was for them to join forces, they nominated Clinton for President and <a href="/wiki/Jared_Ingersoll" title="Jared Ingersoll">Jared Ingersoll</a>, a Philadelphia lawyer, for vice president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStagg,_2019_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStagg,_2019-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Hoping to shore up his support in the Northeast, where the War of 1812 was unpopular, Madison selected Governor <a href="/wiki/Elbridge_Gerry" title="Elbridge Gerry">Elbridge Gerry</a> of Massachusetts as his running mate,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills2002115–116_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills2002115–116-211">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> though Gerry would only survive two years after the election due to advanced old age.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBillias1976323_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBillias1976323-212">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Despite the maneuverings of Clinton and the Federalists, Madison won re-election, though by the narrowest margin of any election since that of 1800 in the popular vote as later supported by the electoral vote as well. He received 128 electoral votes to 89 for Clinton.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE1812_Presidential_Election:_American_Presidency_Project_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE1812_Presidential_Election:_American_Presidency_Project-213">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> With Clinton winning most of the Northeast, Madison won Pennsylvania in addition to having swept the South and the West which ensured his victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009682–683_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009682–683-214">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017555–557_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017555–557-215">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:British_Burning_Washington.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="British action against Madison in 1814." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/British_Burning_Washington.jpg/220px-British_Burning_Washington.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="153" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/British_Burning_Washington.jpg/330px-British_Burning_Washington.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/British_Burning_Washington.jpg/440px-British_Burning_Washington.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="626" /></a><figcaption>The British set ablaze the U.S. Capital while Madison was President on August 24, 1814.</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the disastrous start to the war, Madison accepted Russia's invitation to arbitrate and sent a delegation led by Gallatin and <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> (the first son of former President John Adams) to Europe to negotiate a peace treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills200297–98_202-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills200297–98-202">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> While Madison worked to end the war, the United States experienced some impressive naval successes, by the <a href="/wiki/USS_Constitution" title="USS Constitution">USS&#160;<i>Constitution</i></a> and other warships, that boosted American morale.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017554,_566–567_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017554,_566–567-216">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265_206-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200265-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Victorious in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lake_Erie" title="Battle of Lake Erie">Battle of Lake Erie</a>, the U.S. crippled the supply and reinforcement of British military forces in the western theater of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoosevelt1999147–152_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoosevelt1999147–152-217">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> In the aftermath of the Battle of Lake Erie, General <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> defeated the forces of the British and of Tecumseh's confederacy at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Thames" title="Battle of the Thames">Battle of the Thames</a>. The death of <a href="/wiki/Tecumseh" title="Tecumseh">Tecumseh</a> in that battle marked the permanent end of armed Native American resistance in the Old Northwest and any hope of a united Indian nation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990133_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990133-218">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> In March 1814, Major General <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> broke the resistance of the British-allied <a href="/wiki/Muscogee" title="Muscogee">Muscogee</a> Creek in the Old Southwest with his victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Horseshoe_Bend_(1814)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Horseshoe Bend (1814)">Battle of Horseshoe Bend</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990138–139,_150_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990138–139,_150-219">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Despite those successes, the British continued to repel American attempts to invade Canada, and a British force captured <a href="/wiki/Capture_of_Fort_Niagara" title="Capture of Fort Niagara">Fort Niagara</a> and burned the American city of <a href="/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York" title="Buffalo, New York">Buffalo</a> in late 1813.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017569–571_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017569–571-220">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Military action in New Orleans under Madison." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg/220px-Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg/330px-Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg/440px-Battle_of_New_Orleans_Jean-Hyacinthe_Laclotte.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1055" data-file-height="854" /></a><figcaption>The Battle of New Orleans took place while the Treaty of Ghent was being negotiated in 1815.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On August 24, 1814, the British landed a large force on the shores of <a href="/wiki/Chesapeake_Bay" title="Chesapeake Bay">Chesapeake Bay</a> and routed General <a href="/wiki/William_H._Winder" title="William H. Winder">William Winder</a>'s army at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Bladensburg" title="Battle of Bladensburg">Battle of Bladensburg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017579–585_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017579–585-221">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Madison, who had earlier inspected Winder's army,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021346_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021346-222">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> escaped British capture by fleeing to Virginia on a fresh horse, though the British <a href="/wiki/Burning_of_Washington" title="Burning of Washington">captured Washington</a> and burned many of its buildings, including the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017586–588_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017586–588-223">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200267_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200267-224">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Escaping capture by the British, Dolley had abandoned the capital and fled to Virginia, but only after securing the portrait of <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021346_222-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021346-222">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> The charred remains of the capital signified a humiliating defeat for James Madison and America.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200266–67_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200266–67-225">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> On August 27, Madison returned to Washington to view the carnage of the city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200266–67_225-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200266–67-225">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> Dolley returned the next day, and on September 8, the Madisons moved into <a href="/wiki/The_Octagon_House" title="The Octagon House">the Octagon House</a>. The British army next advanced on <a href="/wiki/Baltimore" title="Baltimore">Baltimore</a>, but the U.S. repelled the British attack in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Baltimore" title="Battle of Baltimore">Battle of Baltimore</a>, and the British army departed from the Chesapeake region in September.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990165–167_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990165–167-226">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> That same month, U.S. forces repelled a British invasion from Canada with a victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Plattsburgh" title="Battle of Plattsburgh">Battle of Plattsburgh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills2002130–131_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills2002130–131-227">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> The British public began to turn against the war in North America, and British leaders began to look for a quick exit from the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990179–180_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990179–180-228">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1815, Jackson's troops defeated the British at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990185_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990185-229">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> Just more than a month later, Madison learned that his negotiators led by John Quincy Adams had concluded the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Ghent" title="Treaty of Ghent">Treaty of Ghent</a> on December 24, 1814, which ended the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_Heritage,_December,_1960_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_Heritage,_December,_1960-230">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison quickly sent the treaty to the Senate, which ratified it on February 16, 1815.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990186–188_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990186–188-231">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> Although the overall result of the war ended in a standoff, the quick succession of events at the end of the war, including the burning of the capital, the Battle of New Orleans, and the Treaty of Ghent, made it appear as though American valor at New Orleans had forced the British to surrender. This view, while inaccurate, strongly contributed to bolstering Madison's reputation as president. Native Americans lost the most, including their land and independence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1987188_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1987188-232">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Napoleon's defeat at the June 1815 <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo" title="Battle of Waterloo">Battle of Waterloo</a> brought a final close to the Napoleonic Wars and thus an end to the hostile seizure of American shipping by British and French forces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990192–201_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990192–201-233">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Postwar_period_and_decline_of_the_Federalist_opposition">Postwar period and decline of the Federalist opposition</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings" title="Era of Good Feelings">Era of Good Feelings</a></div>
<p>The postwar period of Madison's second term saw the transition into the "<a href="/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings" title="Era of Good Feelings">Era of Good Feelings</a>" between mid-1815 and 1817, with the Federalists experiencing a further decline in influence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010547–548_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010547–548-234">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> During the war, delegates from the New England states held the <a href="/wiki/Hartford_Convention" title="Hartford Convention">Hartford Convention</a>, where they asked for several amendments to the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills2002145–150_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills2002145–150-235">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> Though the Hartford Convention did not explicitly call for the secession of New England,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017599–600_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017599–600-236">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> the Convention became an adverse political millstone around the Federalist Party as general American sentiment had moved towards a celebrated unity among the states in what they saw as a successful "second war of independence" from Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990211–212_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990211–212-237">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison hastened the decline of the Federalists by adopting several programs he had previously opposed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010559–560_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010559–560-238">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> Recognizing the difficulties of financing the war and the necessity of an institution to regulate American currency, Madison proposed the re-establishment of a national bank. He also called for increased spending on the army and the navy, a tariff designed to <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protect</a> American goods from foreign competition, and a constitutional amendment authorizing the federal government to fund the construction of <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a> such as roads and canals. Madison's initiatives to now act on behalf of a national bank appeared to reverse his earlier opposition to Hamilton and were opposed by strict constructionists such as John Randolph, who stated that Madison's proposals now "out-Hamiltons Alexander Hamilton".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990195–198_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990195–198-239">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> Responding to Madison's proposals, the <a href="/wiki/14th_United_States_Congress" title="14th United States Congress">14th Congress</a> compiled one of the most productive legislative records up to that point in history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe200782–84_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe200782–84-240">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Congress granted the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> a twenty-five-year charter<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990195–198_239-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990195–198-239">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> and passed the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1816" title="Tariff of 1816">Tariff of 1816</a>, which set high import duties for all goods that were produced outside the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe200782–84_240-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe200782–84-240">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Madison approved federal spending on the <a href="/wiki/Cumberland_Road" class="mw-redirect" title="Cumberland Road">Cumberland Road</a>, which provided a link to the country's western lands;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990198–199_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990198–199-241">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> still, in his last act before leaving office, he blocked further federal spending on internal improvements by vetoing the <a href="/wiki/Bonus_Bill_of_1817" title="Bonus Bill of 1817">Bonus Bill of 1817</a> arguing that it unduly exceeded the limits of the <a href="/wiki/Taxing_and_Spending_Clause" title="Taxing and Spending Clause">General Welfare Clause</a> concerning such improvements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990204–207_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990204–207-242">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Native_American_policy">Native American policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Fort_Wayne_(1809)" title="Treaty of Fort Wayne (1809)">Treaty of Fort Wayne (1809)</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tecumseh%27s_War" title="Tecumseh&#39;s War">Tecumseh's War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tippecanoe.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Tippecanoe and Native American Policy under Madison." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Tippecanoe.jpg/220px-Tippecanoe.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Tippecanoe.jpg/330px-Tippecanoe.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c5/Tippecanoe.jpg 2x" data-file-width="406" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption>The Battle of Tippecanoe took place in the Northwest Territory on <i>November 7, 1811</i>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Upon becoming president, Madison said the federal government's duty was to convert Native Americans by the "participation of the improvements of which the human mind and manners are susceptible in a civilized state".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199020_194-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199020-194">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> In 1809, General Harrison began to push for a treaty to open more land for white American settlement. The Miami, Wea, and Kickapoo were vehemently opposed to selling any more land around the <a href="/wiki/Wabash_River" title="Wabash River">Wabash River</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwens2007200_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwens2007200-243">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> To motivate those groups to sell their land, Harrison decided, against the wishes of Madison, to first conclude a treaty with the tribes who were willing to sell and use those treaties to help influence those who held out. In September 1809, Harrison invited the Potawatomie, Delaware, Eel Rivers, and the Miami to a meeting in <a href="/wiki/Fort_Wayne,_Indiana" title="Fort Wayne, Indiana">Fort Wayne</a>. During the negotiations, Harrison promised large subsidies and direct payments to the tribes if they would cede the other lands under discussion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwens2007201–203_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwens2007201–203-244">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> On September 30, 1809, little more than six months into his first term, Madison agreed to the Treaty of Fort Wayne, negotiated and signed by <a href="/wiki/Indiana_Territory" title="Indiana Territory">Indiana Territory</a>'s Governor Harrison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKappler1904101–102_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKappler1904101–102-245">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> In the treaty, the American Indian tribes were compensated $5,200 (equivalent to $90,092.77&#32;in 2021) in goods and $500 in cash (equivalent to $8,662.77&#32;in 2021), with $250 in annual payments (equivalent to $4,331.38&#32;in 2021), in return for the cession of 3&#160;million acres of land (approximately 12,140 square kilometers) with incentivized subsidies paid to individual tribes for exerting their influence over less cooperative tribes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwens2007201–203Kappler1904101–102_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwens2007201–203Kappler1904101–102-246">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwens2007205_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwens2007205-247">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> The treaty angered Shawnee leader Tecumseh, who said, "Sell a country! Why not sell the air, the clouds and the great sea, as well as the earth?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandry,_2016,_essay_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandry,_2016,_essay-248">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> Harrison responded that tribes were the owners of their land and could sell it to whomever they wished.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELangguth2006165_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELangguth2006165-249">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Like Jefferson, Madison had a paternalistic attitude toward American Indians, encouraging them to become farmers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland199037_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland199037-250">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> Madison believed the adoption of European-style agriculture would help Native Americans assimilate the values of British–U.S. civilization. As pioneers and settlers moved West into large tracts of <a href="/wiki/Cherokee" title="Cherokee">Cherokee</a>, <a href="/wiki/Choctaw" title="Choctaw">Choctaw</a>, <a href="/wiki/Muscogee_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Muscogee people">Creek</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Chickasaw" title="Chickasaw">Chickasaw</a> territory, Madison ordered the U.S. Army to protect Native lands from intrusion by settlers. This was done to the chagrin of his military commander Andrew Jackson, who wanted Madison to ignore Indian pleas to stop the invasion of their lands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990199–200_251-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990199–200-251">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> Tensions continued to mount between the United States and Tecumseh over the 1809 Treaty of Fort Wayne, which ultimately led to Tecumseh's alliance with the British and the Battle of Tippecanoe, on November 7, 1811, in the Northwest Territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990199–200_251-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990199–200-251">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELangguth2006166–169Owens2007214_252-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELangguth2006166–169Owens2007214-252">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> The divisions among the Native American leaders were bitter and before leaving the discussions, Tecumseh informed Harrison that unless the terms of the negotiated treaty were largely nullified, he would seek an alliance with the British.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELangguth2006166_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELangguth2006166-253">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The situation continued to escalate, eventually leading to the outbreak of hostilities between Tecumseh's followers and American settlers later that year. Tensions continued to rise, leading to the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tippecanoe" title="Battle of Tippecanoe">Battle of Tippecanoe</a> during a period sometimes called <a href="/wiki/Tecumseh%27s_War" title="Tecumseh&#39;s War">Tecumseh's War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELangguth2006167_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELangguth2006167-254">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwens2007214_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwens2007214-255">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> Tecumseh was defeated and Indians were pushed off their tribal lands, replaced entirely by white settlers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1990199–200_251-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1990199–200-251">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELangguth2006166–169Owens2007214_252-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELangguth2006166–169Owens2007214-252">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the Battle of the Thames and the Battle of Horseshoe Bend, other wars with American Indians took place, including the <a href="/wiki/Peoria_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Peoria War">Peoria War</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Creek_War" title="Creek War">Creek War</a>. Negotiated by Jackson, in the aftermath of the Creek War, the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Fort_Jackson" title="Treaty of Fort Jackson">Treaty of Fort Jackson</a> of August 9, 1814, added approximately 23&#160;million acres of land to the United States (93,000 square kilometers) in Georgia and Alabama.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreen198243_256-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreen198243-256">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200526_257-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200526-257">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Privately, Madison did not believe American Indians could be fully assimilated to the values of Euro-American culture. He believed that Native Americans may have been unwilling to make "the transition from the hunter, or even the herdsman state, to the agriculture". Madison feared that Native Americans had too great an influence on the settlers they interacted with, who in his view was "irresistibly attracted by that complete liberty, that freedom from bonds, obligations, duties, that absence of care and anxiety which characterize the savage state". Later in Madison's term, in March 1816, Madison's Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/William_H._Crawford" title="William H. Crawford">William Crawford</a> advocated for the government to encourage intermarriages between Native Americans and whites as a way of assimilating the former. This prompted public outrage and exacerbated anti-indigenous bigotry among white Americans, as seen in hostile letters sent to Madison, who remained publicly silent on the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandry,_2016,_essay_248-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandry,_2016,_essay-248">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1816">Election of 1816</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1816_United_States_presidential_election" title="1816 United States presidential election">1816 United States presidential election</a></div>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1816_United_States_presidential_election" title="1816 United States presidential election">1816 presidential election</a>, Madison and Jefferson both favored the candidacy of Secretary of State James Monroe, who defeated Secretary of War William H. Crawford in the party's congressional nominating caucus. As the Federalist Party continued to collapse, Monroe easily defeated Federalist candidate, New York Senator <a href="/wiki/Rufus_King" title="Rufus King">Rufus King</a>, in the 1816 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010559–563_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010559–563-258">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> Madison left office as a popular president; former president Adams wrote that Madison had "acquired more glory, and established more union, than all his three predecessors, Washington, Adams, and Jefferson, put together".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017616_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017616-259">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281817.E2.80.931836.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1817–1836)">Post-presidency (1817–1836)</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gilbert_Stuart,_James_Madison,_c._1821,_NGA_56914.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Stuart portrait of Madison in 1821." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Gilbert_Stuart%2C_James_Madison%2C_c._1821%2C_NGA_56914.jpg/220px-Gilbert_Stuart%2C_James_Madison%2C_c._1821%2C_NGA_56914.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="260" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Gilbert_Stuart%2C_James_Madison%2C_c._1821%2C_NGA_56914.jpg/330px-Gilbert_Stuart%2C_James_Madison%2C_c._1821%2C_NGA_56914.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Gilbert_Stuart%2C_James_Madison%2C_c._1821%2C_NGA_56914.jpg/440px-Gilbert_Stuart%2C_James_Madison%2C_c._1821%2C_NGA_56914.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3388" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption><i>Portrait of James Madison</i> after the completion of his two terms as president <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1821, by <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Stuart" title="Gilbert Stuart">Gilbert Stuart</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>When Madison left office in 1817 at age 65, he retired to Montpelier, not far from Jefferson's <a href="/wiki/Monticello" title="Monticello">Monticello</a>. As with both Washington and Jefferson, Madison left the presidency a poorer man than when he came in. His plantation experienced a steady financial collapse, due to price declines in tobacco and his stepson's mismanagement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>  In his retirement, Madison occasionally became involved in public affairs, advising Andrew Jackson and other presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010608–609_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010608–609-260">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> He remained out of the public debate over the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri Compromise</a>, though he privately complained about the North's opposition to the extension of slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010578–581_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010578–581-261">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> Madison had warm relations with all four of the major candidates in the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 presidential election</a>, but, like Jefferson, largely stayed out of the race.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010589–591_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010589–591-262">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> During <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Andrew_Jackson" title="Presidency of Andrew Jackson">Jackson's presidency</a>, Madison publicly disavowed the <a href="/wiki/Nullification_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nullification Crisis">Nullification</a> movement and argued that no state had the right to <a href="/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States" title="Secession in the United States">secede</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010603–604_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010603–604-263">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> Madison also helped Jefferson establish the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia" title="University of Virginia">University of Virginia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010585_264-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010585-264">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> In 1826, after the death of Jefferson, Madison was appointed as the second rector of the university. He retained the position as college chancellor for ten years until his death in 1836.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas_Jefferson_Foundation,_Essay_265-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas_Jefferson_Foundation,_Essay-265">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1829, at the age of 78, Madison was chosen as a representative to the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Constitutional_Convention_of_1829%E2%80%931830" title="Virginia Constitutional Convention of 1829–1830">Virginia Constitutional Convention</a> for revision of the commonwealth's constitution. It was his last appearance as a statesman. <a href="/wiki/Apportionment_(politics)" title="Apportionment (politics)">Apportionment</a> of adequate representation was the central issue at the convention for the western districts of Virginia. The increased population in the Piedmont and western parts of the state were not proportionately represented in the legislature. Western reformers also wanted to extend suffrage to all white men, in place of the prevailing property ownership requirement. Madison made modest gains but was disappointed at the failure of Virginians to extend suffrage to all white men.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKeysaar200926–27_266-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeysaar200926–27-266">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> In his later years, Madison became highly concerned about his historical legacy. He resorted to modifying letters and other documents in his possession, changing days and dates, and adding and deleting words and sentences. By his late seventies, Madison's self-editing of his own archived letters and older materials had become almost an obsession. As an example, he edited a letter written to Jefferson criticizing <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_du_Motier,_Marquis_de_Lafayette" title="Gilbert du Motier, Marquis de Lafayette">Gilbert du Motier, Marquis de Lafayette</a>; Madison not only inked out original passages but in other correspondence he even forged Jefferson's handwriting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills2002162_267-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills2002162-267">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Drew_R._McCoy" title="Drew R. McCoy">Drew R. McCoy</a> wrote that "During the final six years of his life, amid a sea of personal [financial] troubles that were threatening to engulf him&#160;... At times mental agitation issued in physical collapse. For the better part of a year in 1831 and 1832 he was bedridden, if not silenced&#160;... Literally sick with anxiety, he began to despair of his ability to make himself understood by his fellow citizens."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989151_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989151-268">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span></h3>
<p>Madison's health slowly deteriorated through the early-to-mid-1830s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas_Jefferson_Encyclopedia_269-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas_Jefferson_Encyclopedia-269">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> Approaching the Fourth of July, he died of <a href="/wiki/Congestive_heart_failure" class="mw-redirect" title="Congestive heart failure">congestive heart failure</a> at Montpelier on the morning of June 28, 1836, at the age of 85.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990669–670_270-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990669–670-270">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> According to one common account of his final moments, he was given his breakfast, which he tried eating but was unable to swallow. His favorite niece, who sat by to keep him company, asked him, "What is the matter, Uncle James?" Madison died immediately after he replied, "Nothing more than a change of mind, my dear."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagnet2013321–322_271-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagnet2013321–322-271">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> He was buried in the family cemetery at Montpelier.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison_12-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontpellier,_Life_of_James_Madison-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>  He was one of the last prominent members of the Revolutionary War generation to die.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010608–609_260-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010608–609-260">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> His <a href="/wiki/Last_will_and_testament" class="mw-redirect" title="Last will and testament">last will and testament</a> left significant sums to the <a href="/wiki/American_Colonization_Society" title="American Colonization Society">American Colonization Society</a>, Princeton, and the University of Virginia, as well as $30,000 ($897,000 in 2021) to his wife, Dolley. Left with a smaller sum than James had intended, Dolley suffered financial troubles until her death in 1849.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010609–611_272-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010609–611-272">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> In the 1840s Dolley sold Montpelier, its remaining slaves, and the furnishings to pay off outstanding debts. <a href="/wiki/Paul_Jennings_(slave)" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul Jennings (slave)">Paul Jennings</a>, one of Madison's younger slaves, later recalled in his memoir,
</p>
<blockquote><p>In the last days of her life, before Congress purchased her husband's papers, she was in a state of absolute poverty, and I think sometimes suffered for the necessaries of life. While I was a servant to Mr. Webster, he often sent me to her with a market-basket full of provisions, and told me whenever I saw anything in the house that I thought she was in need of, to take it to her. I often did this, and occasionally gave her small sums from my own pocket, though I had years before bought my freedom of her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJennings,_1865_273-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJennings,_1865-273">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_and_religious_views">Political and religious views</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Federalism">Federalism</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox" style="width: 220px; float: right; clear: right; margin:0 0 1.5em 1.5em"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:115%">External video</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="text-align: left"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="video icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/16px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/24px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/32px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="128" data-file-height="128" /></span></span> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.c-span.org/video/?68744-1/sacred-fire-liberty-james-madison"><i>Booknotes</i> interview with Lance Banning on <i>The Sacred Fire of Liberty: James Madison and the Founding of the Federal Republic</i>, February 11, 1996</a>, <a href="/wiki/C-SPAN" title="C-SPAN">C-SPAN</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>During his first stint in Congress in the 1780s, Madison came to favor amending the Articles of Confederation to provide for a stronger central government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201085–86_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201085–86-274">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> In the 1790s, he led the opposition to Hamilton's centralizing policies and the Alien and Sedition Acts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010232–234_275-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010232–234-275">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> Madison's support of the Virginia and Kentucky Resolutions in the 1790s has been referred to as "a breathtaking evolution for a man who had pleaded at the Constitutional Convention that the federal government should possess a veto over state laws".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChernow2004571–74_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernow2004571–74-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
