<p>Although baptized as an Anglican and educated by <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> clergymen,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman20177_276-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman20177-276">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> young Madison was an avid reader of English <a href="/wiki/Deist" class="mw-redirect" title="Deist">deist</a> tracts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoffer2006363_277-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoffer2006363-277">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> As an adult, Madison paid little attention to religious matters. Though most historians have found little indication of his religious leanings after he left college,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutson2003156_278-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutson2003156-278">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> some scholars indicate he leaned toward deism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiroff2011149_279-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiroff2011149-279">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECorbett201378_280-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECorbett201378-280">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> Others maintain that Madison accepted Christian tenets and formed his outlook on life with a Christian worldview.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham199047_281-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham199047-281">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup> Regardless of his own religious beliefs, Madison believed in religious liberty, and he advocated for Virginia's disestablishment of religious institutions sponsored by the state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010106–107_282-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010106–107-282">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup> He also opposed the appointments of <a href="/wiki/Chaplain" title="Chaplain">chaplains</a> for Congress and the armed forces, arguing that the appointments produce religious exclusion as well as political disharmony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMadison,_Detached_Memoranda,_1817_283-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadison,_Detached_Memoranda,_1817-283">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMadison1908432_284-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadison1908432-284">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Slavery">Slavery</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_and_slavery" title="James Madison and slavery">James Madison and slavery</a></div>
<p>Throughout his life, Madison's views on slavery were conflicted. He was born into a plantation society that relied on slave labor, and both sides of his family profited from tobacco farming.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013_285-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013-285">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> While he viewed slavery as essential to the Southern economy, he was troubled by the instability of a society that depended on a large slave population.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201026,_200–202_286-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg201026,_200–202-286">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup> Madison also believed slavery was incompatible with American Revolutionary principles, though he owned over one hundred <a href="/wiki/African_American" class="mw-redirect" title="African American">African American</a> slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013_285-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013-285">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h3>
<p>Madison grew up on Montpelier, his family's plantation in Virginia. Like other southern plantations, Montpelier depended on slave labor. When Madison left for college on August 10, 1769, he arrived at <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton</a> accompanied by his slave Sawney, who was charged with Madison's expenses and with relaying messages to his family back home.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013_285-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013-285">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> In 1783, fearing the possibility of a slave rebellion at Montpelier, Madison emancipated one slave, <a href="/wiki/William_Gardner_(former_slave)" title="William Gardner (former slave)">Billey</a>, selling him into a seven-year apprentice contract. After his manumission, Billey changed his name to William Gardner, married and had a family,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECost2021255_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECost2021255-287">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup> and became a shipping agent, representing Madison in Philadelphia. In 1795, Gardner was swept overboard and drowned on a voyage to <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatts19901289Ketcham1990374–375Taylor201227_288-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatts19901289Ketcham1990374–375Taylor201227-288">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup>
<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013French2001New_York_Review_of_Books,_June_6,_2019_289-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013French2001New_York_Review_of_Books,_June_6,_2019-289">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> Madison inherited Montpelier and its more than one hundred slaves, after his father's death in 1801.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg20104,_26_290-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg20104,_26-290">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> That same year, Madison was appointed Secretary of State by President Jefferson, and he moved to Washington D.C., running Montpelier from afar making no effort to free his slaves. After his election to the Presidency in 1808, Madison brought his slaves to the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013_285-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013-285">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> During the 1820s and 1830s, Madison sold some of his land and slaves to repay debt. In 1836, at the time of Madison's death, he owned 36 taxable slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBroadwater2012188_291-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroadwater2012188-291">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> In his will, Madison gave his remaining slaves to his wife Dolley and charged her not to sell the slaves without their permission. For reasons of necessity, Dolley did not comply and sold the slaves without their permission to pay off debts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013_285-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013-285">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Treatment_of_slaves">Treatment of slaves</span></h3>
<p>As was consistent with the "established social norms of Virginia society,"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHopkins,_2019_292-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHopkins,_2019-292">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> Madison was known from his farm papers for advocating the humane treatment of his slaves at Montpelier. He instructed an overseer to "treat the Negroes with all the humanity and kindness consistent with their necessary subordination and work."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010201_293-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010201-293">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup> Madison also ensured that his slaves had milk cows and meals for their daily food.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990374–375_294-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990374–375-294">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> By the 1790s, Madison's slave Sawney was an overseer of part of the plantation. Madison ordered Sawney by letter to ready fields for growing apples, corn, tobacco, and Irish potatoes. Like Sawney, some slaves at Montpelier could read.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990374–375_294-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990374–375-294">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> Enslaved people at Montpelier worked six days a week from dawn to dusk, with a mid-day break, and got Sundays off.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHopkins,_2019Jennings,_1865_295-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHopkins,_2019Jennings,_1865-295">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor201222Ketcham1990374–375_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor201222Ketcham1990374–375-296">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Paul_Jennings_(abolitionist)" title="Paul Jennings (abolitionist)">Paul Jennings</a> was a slave of the Madisons for 48 years. Jennings, born into slavery in 1799 at the Montpelier plantation, served as Madison's footman at the White House. In his memoir <i>A Colored Man's Reminiscences of James Madison</i>, published in 1865, Jennings said that he "never knew [Madison] to strike a slave, although he had over one hundred; neither would he allow an overseer to do it." As a house slave, Jennings had a basic education and was literate, taught in <a href="/wiki/Mathematics" title="Mathematics">mathematics</a>, and played the violin. Although Jennings condemned slavery, he said that James was "one of the best men that ever lived", and that Dolley was "a remarkably fine woman."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Park_Service_2022_297-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Park_Service_2022-297">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor201222_298-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor201222-298">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Views_on_slavery">Views on slavery</span></h3>
<p>Madison called slavery "the most oppressive dominion" that ever existed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017121_299-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017121-299">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> and he had a "lifelong abhorrence" for it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990149_300-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990149-300">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup> In 1785 Madison spoke in the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Assembly" class="mw-redirect" title="Virginia Assembly">Virginia Assembly</a> favoring a bill that <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> had proposed for the <a href="/wiki/Gradual_emancipation_(United_States)" title="Gradual emancipation (United States)">gradual abolition of slavery</a>, and he also helped defeat a bill designed to outlaw the <a href="/wiki/Manumission" title="Manumission">manumission</a> of individual slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990149_300-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham1990149-300">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup> As a slaveholder, Madison was aware that owning slaves was not consistent with revolutionary values,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017p._52:_&quot;Madison_knew_that_the_institution_of_slavery_contradicted_the_humanitarian_ideals_of_the_Revolution._His_livelihood,_then_and_throughout_his_life,_depended_on_slavery&amp;nbsp;..._He_was_not_trying_to_introduce_abolition_by_subtleties._Madison_was_attempting_the_doubtful,_self-contradictory_goal_of_being_a_humane_slaveholder,_one_actuated_by_revolutionary_principles&quot;_301-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017p._52:_&quot;Madison_knew_that_the_institution_of_slavery_contradicted_the_humanitarian_ideals_of_the_Revolution._His_livelihood,_then_and_throughout_his_life,_depended_on_slavery&amp;nbsp;..._He_was_not_trying_to_introduce_abolition_by_subtleties._Madison_was_attempting_the_doubtful,_self-contradictory_goal_of_being_a_humane_slaveholder,_one_actuated_by_revolutionary_principles&quot;-301">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup> but, as a <a href="/wiki/Pragmatism" title="Pragmatism">pragmatist</a>, this sort of self-contradiction was a common feature in his political career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman201716–17_302-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman201716–17-302">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Drew_R._McCoy" title="Drew R. McCoy">Drew R. McCoy</a> said that Madison's <a href="/wiki/Antislavery" class="mw-redirect" title="Antislavery">antislavery</a> principles were indeed "impeccable."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989260_303-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989260-303">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup>  Historian <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Ketcham" title="Ralph Ketcham">Ralph Ketcham</a> said, "[a]lthough Madison abhorred slavery, he nonetheless bore the burden of depending all his life on a slave system that he could never square with his republican beliefs."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257_9-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKetcham200257-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> There is no evidence Madison thought <a href="/wiki/Scientific_racism" title="Scientific racism">black people were inferior</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERutland1987241_304-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERutland1987241-304">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017121_299-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017121-299">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> Madison believed <a href="/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States" title="Racial segregation in the United States">blacks and whites were unlikely to co-exist peacefully</a> due to "the prejudices of the whites" as well as feelings on both sides "inspired by their former relation as oppressors and oppressed."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017284_305-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017284-305">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup> As such, he became interested in the idea of <a href="/wiki/Freedman" title="Freedman">freedmen</a> establishing colonies in <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a> and later served as the president of the <a href="/wiki/American_Colonization_Society" title="American Colonization Society">American Colonization Society</a>, which relocated former slaves to <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010200–201,_607–608_306-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010200–201,_607–608-306">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup> Madison believed that this solution offered a gradual, long-term, but potentially feasible means of <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">eradicating slavery in the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCoy19895_307-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCoy19895-307">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup> Madison nevertheless thought that peaceful co-existence between the two racial groups could eventually be achieved in the long run.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010201_293-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010201-293">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madison initially opposed the Constitution's 20-year protection of the foreign <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">slave trade</a>, but he eventually accepted it as a necessary compromise to get the South to ratify the document.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJames_Madison_Memorial_Foundation_308-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJames_Madison_Memorial_Foundation-308">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup> He also proposed that apportionment in the House of Representatives be according to each state's free and enslaved population, eventually leading to the adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Three-fifths_Compromise" title="Three-fifths Compromise">Three-fifths Compromise</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010156–157_309-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010156–157-309">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup> Madison supported the extension of slavery into the West during the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri crisis of 1819–1821</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENew_York_Review_of_Books,_June_6,_2019_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENew_York_Review_of_Books,_June_6,_2019-310">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> asserting that the spread of slavery would not lead to more slaves, but rather diminish their generative increase through dispersing them,<sup id="cite_ref-312" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-312">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup> thus substantially improving their condition, accelerating <a href="/wiki/Emancipation" title="Emancipation">emancipation</a>, easing <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_conflict" title="Ethnic conflict">racial tensions</a>, and increasing "partial manumissions."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989268–269_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989268–269-313">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup> Madison thought of slaves as "wayward (but still educable) students in need of regular guidance."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010201_293-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010201-293">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup> According to historian Paris Spies-Gans, Madison's anti-slavery thought was strongest "at the height of Revolutionary politics. But by the early 1800s, when in a position to truly impact policy, he failed to follow through on these views." Spies-Gans concluded, "[u]ltimately, Madison's personal dependence on slavery led him to question his own, once enlightened, definition of liberty itself."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013_285-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpies-Gans,_2013-285">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation">Historical reputation</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Madison portrait in advanced age." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg/220px-James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="245" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg/330px-James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg/440px-James_madison-Age82-Edit1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1812" data-file-height="2014" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Madison after his health began to fail, age 82, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1833</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Regarded as one of the <a href="/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States" title="Founding Fathers of the United States">Founding Fathers of the United States</a>, Madison had a wide influence on the founding of the nation and upon the early development of American constitutional government and foreign policy. Historian J.C.A. Stagg writes that "in some ways—because he was on the winning side of every important issue facing the young nation from 1776 to 1816—Madison was the most successful and possibly the most influential of all the Founding Fathers."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStagg,_2019_51-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStagg,_2019-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Though he helped found a major political party and served as the fourth president, his legacy has largely been defined by his contributions to the Constitution; even in his own life he was hailed as the "Father of the Constitution".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017625–626_314-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017625–626-314">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup> Law professor Noah Feldman writes that Madison "invented and theorized the modern ideal of an expanded, federal constitution that combines local self-government with an overarching national order".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017627–628_315-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017627–628-315">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup> Feldman adds that Madison's "model of liberty-protecting constitutional government" is "the most influential American idea in global political history".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017627–628_315-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017627–628-315">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-317" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-317">&#91;i&#93;</a></sup> Various rankings of historians and political scientists tend to <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">rank</a> Madison as an above average president with a 2018 poll of the <a href="/wiki/American_Political_Science_Association" title="American Political Science Association">American Political Science Association</a>'s Presidents and Executive Politics section ranking Madison as the twelfth best president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENew_York_Review_of_Books,_June_6,_2019_310-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENew_York_Review_of_Books,_June_6,_2019-310">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Various historians have criticized Madison's tenure as president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkidmore200445–56_318-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkidmore200445–56-318">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> In 1968, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Steele_Commager" title="Henry Steele Commager">Henry Steele Commager</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richard_B._Morris" title="Richard B. Morris">Richard B. Morris</a> said the conventional view of Madison was of an "incapable President" who "mismanaged an unnecessary war".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmelser,_1968,_introduction_by_Commager_&amp;_Morris_319-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmelser,_1968,_introduction_by_Commager_&amp;_Morris-319">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> A 2006 poll of historians ranked Madison's failure to prevent the War of 1812 as the sixth-worst mistake made by a sitting president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUSA_Today,_February_18,_2006_320-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSA_Today,_February_18,_2006-320">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> Regarding Madison's consistency and adaptability of policy-making during his many years of political activity, historian <a href="/wiki/Gordon_S._Wood" title="Gordon S. Wood">Gordon S. Wood</a> says that Lance Banning, as in his <i>Sacred Fire of Liberty</i> (1995), is the "only present-day scholar to maintain that Madison did not change his views in the 1790s".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2006425_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2006425-321">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During and after the War of 1812, Madison came to support several of the policies that he opposed in the 1790s, including the national bank, a strong navy, and direct taxes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010521–522_322-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBursteinIsenberg2010521–522-322">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> Wood notes that many historians struggle to understand Madison, but Wood looks at him in the terms of Madison's own times—as a nationalist but one with a different conception of nationalism than that of the Federalists.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2006425_321-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2006425-321">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> Gary Rosen uses other approaches to suggest Madison's consistency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERosen19992–4,_6–9,_140–175_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERosen19992–4,_6–9,_140–175-323">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Garry_Wills" title="Garry Wills">Garry Wills</a> wrote, "Madison's claim on our admiration does not rest on a perfect consistency, any more than it rests on his presidency. He has other virtues. &#160;... As a framer and defender of the Constitution he had no peer. &#160;... No man could do everything for the country—not even Washington. Madison did more than most, and did some things better than any. That was quite enough."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWills2002164_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWills2002164-324">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Popular_culture">Popular culture</span></h3>
<p>Madison, portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Burgess_Meredith" title="Burgess Meredith">Burgess Meredith</a>, is a key protagonist in the 1946 Hollywood film <i><a href="/wiki/Magnificent_Doll" title="Magnificent Doll">Magnificent Doll</a></i>, which focuses on a fictionalized account of Dolley Madison's romantic life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Cincinnati_Post,November_28,_1946,_p._23_325-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Cincinnati_Post,November_28,_1946,_p._23-325">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> Madison is also portrayed in the popular musical <i><a href="/wiki/Hamilton_(musical)" title="Hamilton (musical)">Hamilton</a></i>, played by <a href="/wiki/Joshua_Henry" title="Joshua Henry">Joshua Henry</a> in the original 2013 Vassar version and then revised by <a href="/wiki/Okieriete_Onaodowan" title="Okieriete Onaodowan">Okieriete Onaodowan</a> for the 2015 Broadway opening.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAIM_Media_Midwest,_July_17,_2015_326-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIM_Media_Midwest,_July_17,_2015-326">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHollywood_Reporter,_July_28,_2015_327-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHollywood_Reporter,_July_28,_2015-327">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBroadwayWorld,_October_2,_2015_328-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroadwayWorld,_October_2,_2015-328">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> In the Broadway musical, Madison, joined by Jefferson and Burr, confront Hamilton about his <i>affaire de cœur</i> in the <a href="/wiki/Hamilton%E2%80%93Reynolds_affair" title="Hamilton–Reynolds affair">Reynolds affair</a> by intoning the rap lyrics to the song "We Know". Onaodowan won a <a href="/wiki/Grammy_Award" class="mw-redirect" title="Grammy Award">Grammy Award</a> for his portrayal of Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECBS_News,_December_12,_2015_329-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECBS_News,_December_12,_2015-329">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/List_of_memorials_to_James_Madison" title="List of memorials to James Madison">List of memorials to James Madison</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Montpelier_(Orange,_Virginia)" title="Montpelier (Orange, Virginia)">Montpelier</a>, the Madison family's plantation, has been designated a <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark" title="National Historic Landmark">National Historic Landmark</a>. The <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_Memorial_Building" title="James Madison Memorial Building">James Madison Memorial Building</a> is part of the United States <a href="/wiki/Library_of_Congress" title="Library of Congress">Library of Congress</a> and serves as the official memorial to Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Capital_Planning_Commission_330-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Capital_Planning_Commission-330">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup>  In 1986, Congress created the <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_Memorial_Fellowship_Foundation" title="James Madison Memorial Fellowship Foundation">James Madison Memorial Fellowship Foundation</a> as part of the bicentennial celebration of the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Capital_Planning_Commission2_331-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Capital_Planning_Commission2-331">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Courier-Journal,_p._b3_332-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Courier-Journal,_p._b3-332">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup> Other memorials include <a href="/wiki/Madison,_Wisconsin" title="Madison, Wisconsin">Madison, Wisconsin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Madison_County,_Alabama" title="Madison County, Alabama">Madison County, Alabama</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReal_Time_Alabama_July_17,_2020_333-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReal_Time_Alabama_July_17,_2020-333">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGannett1905196_334-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGannett1905196-334">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> which were both named for Madison, as were <a href="/wiki/Madison_Square_Garden" title="Madison Square Garden">Madison Square Garden</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_University" title="James Madison University">James Madison University</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/USS_James_Madison_(SSBN-627)" class="mw-redirect" title="USS James Madison (SSBN-627)">USS <i>James Madison</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMendelsohn1995711–712_335-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendelsohn1995711–712-335">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWisconsin_Historical_Society_336-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWisconsin_Historical_Society-336">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECIA_memo,_25_January_2017_337-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECIA_memo,_25_January_2017-337">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup> In 2021, the <a href="/wiki/Madison_Metropolitan_School_District" title="Madison Metropolitan School District">Madison Metropolitan School District</a> renamed <a href="/wiki/James_Madison_Memorial_High_School" class="mw-redirect" title="James Madison Memorial High School">James Madison Memorial High School</a> following community opposition to commemorating someone who used slave labor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJournal_Sentinel_338-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJournal_Sentinel-338">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-columns references-column-width reflist-lower-alpha reflist-columns-2">
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-vp-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-vp_3-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-vp_3-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">Vice President Clinton and Vice President Gerry both died in office. Neither was replaced for the remainder of their respective terms, as the Constitution did not have a provision for filling a vice presidential vacancy prior to the adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twenty-fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twenty-fifth Amendment</a> in 1967.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBillias1976329_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBillias1976329-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-8">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> March 5, 1750.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-50"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-50">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">After the ratification of the Articles of Confederation in 1781, the Second Continental Congress became the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_the_Confederation" title="Congress of the Confederation">Congress of the Confederation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerling2003230–232_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerling2003230–232-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-114"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-114">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Portions of the Bill of Rights would later be <a href="/wiki/Incorporation_of_the_Bill_of_Rights" title="Incorporation of the Bill of Rights">incorporated</a> against the states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELabunski2006198_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELabunski2006198-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-117"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-117">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">One of the two unratified amendments became part of the Constitution in 1992 as the <a href="/wiki/Twenty-seventh_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twenty-seventh Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twenty-seventh Amendment</a>. The other unratified amendment, known as the <a href="/wiki/Congressional_Apportionment_Amendment" title="Congressional Apportionment Amendment">Congressional Apportionment Amendment</a>, is technically still pending before the states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas201349_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas201349-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-126"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-126">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The Democratic–Republican Party was often referred to as the "Republican Party". It was a separate entity from the later <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican Party</a>, which was founded in the 1850s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2012405–406_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2012405–406-125">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-129"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-129">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Because the Constitution required presidential electors to vote for at least one individual from outside their home state, electors from Virginia would not have been able to vote for both Washington and Jefferson.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017369_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017369-128">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-312"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-312">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to <a href="/wiki/Drew_R._McCoy" title="Drew R. McCoy">McCoy</a>, through this reasoning, Madison implicitly rejected "the <a href="/wiki/Malthusianism" title="Malthusianism">Malthusian logic of restrictionists</a>, who contended that diffusion, by increasing the supply of available subsistence to the black population, would indeed increase their numbers by accelerating the rate of natural growth."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989268_311-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCoy1989268-311">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-317"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-317">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Historian Gordon Wood commends Madison for his steady leadership during the war and resolve to avoid expanding the president's power, noting one admirer's observation that the war was conducted "without one trial for treason, or even one prosecution for libel".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2009697–699_316-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2009697–699-316">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
</ol></div>

<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017p._52:_&quot;Madison_knew_that_the_institution_of_slavery_contradicted_the_humanitarian_ideals_of_the_Revolution._His_livelihood,_then_and_throughout_his_life,_depended_on_slavery&amp;nbsp;..._He_was_not_trying_to_introduce_abolition_by_subtleties._Madison_was_attempting_the_doubtful,_self-contradictory_goal_of_being_a_humane_slaveholder,_one_actuated_by_revolutionary_principles&quot;-301"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman2017p._52:_&quot;Madison_knew_that_the_institution_of_slavery_contradicted_the_humanitarian_ideals_of_the_Revolution._His_livelihood,_then_and_throughout_his_life,_depended_on_slavery&amp;nbsp;..._He_was_not_trying_to_introduce_abolition_by_subtleties._Madison_was_attempting_the_doubtful,_self-contradictory_goal_of_being_a_humane_slaveholder,_one_actuated_by_revolutionary_principles&quot;_301-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFFeldman2017">Feldman 2017</a>, p. 52: "Madison knew that the institution of slavery contradicted the humanitarian ideals of the Revolution. His livelihood, then and throughout his life, depended on slavery&#160;... He was not trying to introduce abolition by subtleties. Madison was attempting the doubtful, self-contradictory goal of being a humane slaveholder, one actuated by revolutionary principles".</span>
